# PREFACE.

A few years ago I spent some time every summer in viewing, measuring, and considering the works of the ancient *Druids* in our *Island* <note>Referring to Great Britain.</note>; I mean those remarkable circles of *Stones* which we find all over the kingdom, many of which I have seen, but of many more I have had accounts. Their greatness and number astonished me, nor need I be afraid to say, their beauty and design, as well as antiquity, drew my particular attention. I could not help carrying my inquiries about them as far as I was able. My studies this way have produced a vast quantity of drawings and writing, which considered as an *entire* work, may thus be *entitled*,

->Patriarchal CHRISTIANITY:<-
->OR,<-
->A Chronological HISTORY<-
->OF THE<-
->Origin and Progress of true Religion, and of Idolatry.<-

->*The parts of which the whole is composed are these:*<-

I. *The Canon of Mosaic Chronology* <note>original: "Canon Mosaicæ Chronologiæ"</note>, or the *year of Moses settled*, by which he reckons time in the history of the old world; the time of the year fixed when creation was begun. This is done in a new manner, and becomes an entire system of chronology from the creation to the *Exodus*, and is exemplified by many particular *Calendars* of the most remarkable transactions; which are proofs of the truth of the *Canon*. There are interspersed a great many astronomical and historical illustrations of the sacred pages, particularly <term>Sanchoniathon's</term> <note>A Phoenician author whose works survive only in fragments; Stukeley uses him to synchronize pagan and biblical history.</note> genealogies, and <term>Manetho's</term> <note>An Egyptian priest and historian; Stukeley refers to his lists of Egyptian kings.</note> *Egyptian Dynasties*, are applied in a new Method to the history and chronology of the *Scriptures*.

II. *Melchizedek* <note>A mysterious biblical figure, king and priest, whom Stukeley views as a representative of the "true" religion before the era of Jewish Law.</note>, or a delineation of the first and patriarchal religion, from the best light we can gather in the sacred history; and from the most ancient heathen customs, which were remains of that religion. In this Treatise it is shown, that the first religion was no other than Christianity, the *Mosaic* dispensation <note>The religious laws and systems established by Moses.</note>, as a veil, intervening; that all mankind from the creation had a knowledge of the plurality of persons in the Deity <note>A reference to the Christian doctrine of the Trinity being known to ancient patriarchs.</note>.

III. *Of the mysteries of the ancients*, one of the first deviations from true religion, to idolatry; this is chiefly pursued in an explication of the famous <term>Table of Isis</term> <note>Also known as the Bembine Table, an elaborate bronze tablet with Egyptian-style imagery, once thought to be an ancient ritual object.</note>, or *Bembin-table*, published by <term>Pignoria</term> <note>Lorenzo Pignoria (1571–1631), an Italian antiquarian.</note>, <term>Kircher</term> <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit scholar famous for his attempts to decode Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note>, &c. wherein that knowledge which the ancients had concerning the true nature of the Deity, is further explained.

<meta>catchword: IV. A dis-</meta>

## PREFACE.

*IV. A discourse on the <term>hieroglyphic</term> <note>From the Greek for "sacred carvings," referring here to the ancient Egyptian writing system.</note> learning of the ancients, and of the origin of the alphabet of letters. Very many hieroglyphic monuments of the Egyptians are explained, more especially those that relate to their true notions of the persons in the Deity. The time and rise of the alphabet of letters is deduced from a new foundation. The present square Hebrew characters are shown to be the primitive idea of letters, from whence all others are derived. Whence the idea of every letter was taken? an explication of all the old Hebrew coins with <term>Samaritan characters</term> <note>An ancient script closely related to Paleo-Hebrew, used by the Samaritans.</note>.*

*V. The patriarchal history, particularly of Abraham, is largely pursued; and the deduction of the <term>Phoenician colony</term> <note>Stukeley believed that ancient Phoenician traders from the Levant settled in Britain, bringing Eastern wisdom with them.</note> into the Island of Britain, about or soon after his time; whence the origin of the Druids, of their Religion and writing; they brought the patriarchal Religion along with them, and some knowledge of symbols or hieroglyphs, like those of the ancient Egyptians; they had the notion and expectation of the Messiah, and of the time of the year when he was to be born, of his office and death.*

*VI. Of the Temples of the Druids in Britain, their religious rites, orders, sacrifices, groves, tombs, their <term>ancient tracks</term> <note>original: "cursus's" — refers to large, linear Neolithic earthworks found near monuments like Stonehenge.</note>, places of sports and exercises, etc. particularly an ample and accurate description of that stupendous temple of theirs at <term>Abury</term> <note>The modern village of Avebury in Wiltshire, home to the largest stone circle in the world.</note> in North Wiltshire, the most august work at this day upon the globe of the earth; with many prints of ground-plots, views and admeasurements of all its parts; of their manner of <term>sepulture</term> <note>The practice of burial.</note>; an account of my digging into many of their <term>burial mounds</term> <note>original: "barrows and tumuli"</note>, with drawings of them, etc.*

*VII. Of the celebrated Stonehenge, another Temple of theirs, with prints of that work; an account of the burial mounds I dug up, and what was discovered in them; of the knowledge the Druids had of the magnetical compass, and conjectures of the particular times when these works were made, long before Caesar arrived in Britain.*

I propose to publish these two first, and proceed to the speculative parts afterwards; reserving them, God willing, to the maturer time of my life. 
My intent is (besides preserving the memory of these extraordinary monuments, so much to the honour of our country, now in great danger of ruin) to promote, as much as I am able, the knowledge and practice of ancient and true Religion; to revive in the minds of the learned the spirit of Christianity, nearly as old as the Creation, which is now languishing among us; to restore the first and great Idea of the Deity, who has carried on the same regular and golden chain of Religion from the beginning to this day; to warm our hearts into that true sense of Religion, which keeps the medium between ignorant superstition and learned <term>free-thinking</term> <note>In the 18th century, "free-thinking" was often used as a derogatory term for deism or atheism.</note>, between slovenly fanaticism and <term>popish pageantry</term> <note>A common 18th-century Protestant critique of the elaborate rituals of the Roman Catholic Church.</note>, between enthusiasm and the rational worship of God, which is nowhere upon earth done, in my judgment, better than in the Church of England. And seeing a spirit of Scepticism has of late become so fashionable and audacious as to strike at the fundamentals of all revelation, I have endeavoured to trace it back to the fountain of Divinity, whence it flows; and show that Religion is one system as old as the world, and that that is the Christian Religion; that God did not leave the rational part of his creation, like the colony of an ant-hill, with no other guide than instinct, but proportioned his discoveries to the age of the world, to the learning, wisdom, and experience of it; as a wise parent does now to his children. I shall show likewise, that our predecessors, the Druids of Britain, though left in the extremest west to the improvement of their own thoughts, yet advanced their inquiries, under all disadvantages, to such heights, as should make our moderns ashamed, to wink in the sunshine of learning and religion. And we may with reason conclude, there was somewhat very extraordinary in those principles, which prompted them to such a noble spirit as produced these works, still visible with us,

He expresses his desire to use these archaeological findings to defend the Church of England against modern skepticism.</summary>

<meta>This page outlines the author's plan for a multi-volume work connecting Egyptian hieroglyphics, Hebrew origins, and British Druidry to a single, ancient Christian tradition.</meta>

# PREFACE.

*IV. A discourse on the symbolic wisdom <note>original: "hieroglyphic learning"</note> of the ancients and the origin of the alphabet. Many Egyptian monuments are explained, especially those relating to their true understanding of the Holy Trinity <note>original: "persons in the Deity"</note>. The timeline and rise of the alphabet is traced from a new perspective. The current square Hebrew characters are shown to be the original form of letters from which all others are derived. I explore where the idea for every letter was taken, alongside an explanation of all the ancient Hebrew coins that use the Samaritan script.*

*V. The history of the Patriarchs, particularly Abraham, is explored in detail, as is the migration <note>original: "deduction"</note> of a Phoenician colony to the Island of Britain during or shortly after his time. This explains the origin of the Druids, their religion, and their writing; they brought the original religion of the Patriarchs with them, along with knowledge of symbols and hieroglyphs similar to those of the ancient Egyptians. They held a concept and expectation of the Messiah, including the time of year he was to be born, his purpose, and his death.*

*VI. Regarding the Temples of the Druids in Britain: their religious rituals, social ranks, sacrifices, sacred groves, and tombs; their ancient racing tracks <note>original: "cursus’s"</note>, places of sport and exercise, and so on. This includes a full and accurate description of their magnificent temple at Avebury <note>original: "Abury"</note> in North Wiltshire, the most majestic work on the face of the earth today. I include many prints of ground plans, views, and measurements of all its parts, as well as an account of their burial methods and my excavations of many burial mounds <note>original: "barrows and tumuli"</note> with accompanying drawings.*

*VII. Regarding the celebrated Stonehenge, another of their temples, including prints of that work; an account of the burial mounds I excavated and what was discovered in them; the knowledge the Druids had of the magnetic compass; and theories on the specific times these works were constructed, which was long before Caesar arrived in Britain.*

I propose to publish these first two volumes first and proceed to the more theoretical parts later, reserving them—God willing— for a more mature time in my life.

My intention (besides preserving the memory of these extraordinary monuments for the honor of our country, as they are now in danger of being destroyed) is to promote, as much as I am able, the knowledge and practice of true and ancient Religion. I wish to revive in the minds of the learned the spirit of Christianity—which is nearly as old as the Creation itself, yet is now fading among us. I aim to restore the primary idea of the Deity, who has maintained the same consistent and "golden chain" of Religion from the beginning of time to this day. I hope to warm our hearts toward that true sense of Religion which maintains the middle ground: between ignorant superstition and "enlightened" atheism <note>original: "learned free-thinking"</note>; between messy fanaticism and the theatrical rituals of the Roman Catholic Church <note>original: "popish pageantry"</note>; and between irrational religious mania <note>original: "enthusiasm"</note> and the rational worship of God. In my judgment, this balance is achieved nowhere on earth better than in the Church of England.

Since a spirit of Skepticism has lately become so fashionable and bold as to attack the fundamentals of all divine revelation, I have endeavored to trace it back to the fountain of Divinity from which it flows. I aim to show that Religion is a single system as old as the world, and that this system is the Christian Religion. God did not leave the rational part of his creation like a colony of ants with no guide other than instinct; rather, he revealed his truths in proportion to the world's age, learning, wisdom, and experience—just as a wise parent does for his children. I shall also show that our predecessors, the Druids of Britain, though left in the furthest West to develop their own thoughts, advanced their inquiries to such heights (despite all disadvantages) that it should make modern thinkers ashamed to ignore the light of learning and religion. We may reasonably conclude there was something very extraordinary in those principles which prompted them to such a noble spirit—a spirit that produced these works still visible among us,

He details his upcoming archaeological accounts of Avebury and Stonehenge as evidence of this "golden chain" of true religion.</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s introductory argument regarding the origins of the Druids and begins a historical review of previous scholarly attempts to understand Stonehenge.</meta>

# STONEHENGE

<margin>Plate I.</margin>

Because the most significant and pure aspects of their own doctrine were so similar, this soon put an end to their religious governance—something the *Roman* military could not achieve. They embraced that religion <note>Christianity</note> toward which their own opinions and rites had such a natural lean. This was the sentiment of *Origen* <note>An influential early Christian theologian (c. 184–253 AD)</note> in his commentary on *Ezekiel* 4. This is evident enough when we consider that the first missionaries of Christianity in *Ireland* converted the entire island immediately, without the shedding of a single martyr’s blood. Indeed, the Druids themselves—at that time the only national priests—embraced it readily; some became very zealous preachers and effective converters of others. For example, the great *Columbanus* <note>St. Columba (521–597 AD), the "Apostle to the Picts," though the author here identifies him as a Druid</note> himself was a Druid: the apostle of *Ireland*, *Cornwall*, and other regions. We should not be surprised by this if we maintain, as there is much reason to believe, that these famous philosopher-priests arrived here as a *Phoenician* colony in the earliest times, perhaps as soon as the city of *Tyre* was founded—during the life of the patriarch *Abraham*, or very soon after. 

Therefore, they brought with them the "patriarchal religion" <note>The author refers to the faith practiced by Biblical figures like Abraham before the formal Mosaic Law</note>, which was so extremely similar to Christianity that it differed only in this: they believed in a Messiah who was yet to come into the world, just as we believe in him who has already come. Furthermore, they came from the very country where *Abraham*, his sons, and his grandsons lived—a family Almighty God had separated from the rest of mankind to stop the spread of idolatry; a family of mighty princes and preachers of righteousness. And though the records of our Druids are extremely brief, we can clearly discover from them that the Druids followed *Abraham’s* religion entirely, at least in the earliest times. They worshipped the Supreme Being in the same manner he did, and likely followed his example, or the example of their shared ancestors.

I shall prove all of this as I proceed with this work. But before we move to theory, I intend to give an exact description of their various temples and similar structures; such physical evidence will provide a solid foundation for us to build upon. In this way, we may move from things that are evident and well-known to those that are less known, which I intend to make clear as best as the nature of the subject permits. A matter so deeply buried in the dark mist of time, where so few scattered traces remain, must naturally ask for the reader’s fair-mindedness. The importance of the subject will, I hope, excuse my boldness in attempting such a difficult task. However I succeed in explaining these wonderful works, I shall at least be instrumental in preserving their memory by providing accurate drawings of them.

*Stonehenge*, by the extravagant grandeur of its construction, has attracted the attention and admiration of all ages. After the Reformation, when learning was revived among us, the curious began to examine it more closely, though I cannot say they did so successfully. Mr. *Camden* <note>William Camden (1551–1623), a pioneering historian and author of 'Britannia'</note> rose like the sun of antiquity, outshining all previous lights; like *Caesar*, he intimidates anyone who values their reputation from attempting to improve upon his work. Yet his great skill in *Roman* learning and *English* history only enabled him to remain, as it were, silent on *Stonehenge*. He saw with excellent judgment that neither the *Romans* nor the *English* had a hand in it, and thus they could not help explain it. He writes modestly, as was his custom: "Of these things I am not able to give an accurate account, but rather must grieve that the founders of this noble monument cannot be traced." He could not convince himself that the *Romans*, *Saxons*, or *Danes* had any part in it. As for his visual representation of it, I truly believe it was drawn only from imagination or memory, or perhaps by an engraver following his verbal description. 

In *A.D.* 1620, King *James* I, while staying at the Earl of *Pembroke’s* estate at *Wilton*, was agreeably surprised by the sight of *Stonehenge*. He consulted the famous architect *Inigo Jones* about it, thinking it a matter suited to his expertise. This great man, who may rightly be called the *English Vitruvius* <note>A reference to Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the definitive Roman authority on architecture</note>, gave his opinion that it was a *Roman* work. He left behind, I suppose, a few unorganized notes in writing on the subject. From these, his son-in-law *John Webb* composed an entire treatise attempting to prove this theory. But those who are acquainted with *Roman* archi-

<meta>catchword: tecture,</meta>

<summary>The author argues that Druidism was an ancient form of Christianity brought by Phoenician settlers from the Levant. He then transitions to a critique of past scholars like Camden and Inigo Jones, who struggled to identify Stonehenge's true origins.</summary>
<keywords>Druids, Phoenicians, Abraham, Stonehenge history, William Camden, Inigo Jones, Roman architecture, John Webb, patriarchal religion</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of previous antiquarian theories regarding Stonehenge, specifically challenging the idea that it was a Roman or Danish construction, before proposing a Druidical origin.</meta>

-># DESCRIBED.<-

Anyone familiar with architecture, or who has carefully considered *Stonehenge*, must surely hold a different opinion. And as the Lord Bishop of *London* <note>Edmund Gibson (1669–1748), who edited a famous edition of Camden's *Britannia*</note> rightly observes in his notes on *Camden*, "it cannot be safe to agree with *Mr. Jones*, even though his book is otherwise a learned and ingenious work." *Inigo Jones* <note>A famous English architect (1573–1652) who argued Stonehenge was a Roman temple</note> lived 30 years after his study, and yet *Mr. Webb* <note>John Webb, Jones's assistant and relative, who published Jones's notes</note> makes an apology for the work, saying "that if he had survived to finish it with his own hand, it would have been better." But it is very reasonably believed that although *Inigo Jones* was an extraordinary genius in architecture, he lacked many of the qualifications necessary for an author, especially for a work concerning *Stonehenge*. It is my opinion that even if his architectural skill had been joined with *Mr. Camden’s* <note>William Camden (1551–1623), a pioneering historian</note> learning, he could never have proven *Stonehenge* to be a Roman work. 

Afterwards, *Dr. Charlton* <note>Walter Charleton (1619–1707), physician to Charles II</note> published a piece against *Webb’s* work and certainly said enough to overthrow it, though he could not successfully establish his own opinion—that it was the work of the *Danes*. On the contrary, *Olaus Wormius* <note>A Danish physician and antiquary</note> finds no such monuments among the Gothic nations; which, as *Mr. Toland* <note>John Toland (1670–1722), an Irish scholar who wrote on the Druids</note> observes, is a sufficient answer to his claim. *Webb* answered the *Doctor’s* book and, in turn, effectively demolished his opinion, but still could not justify his own. Yet from all their arguments, no spark of light was struck toward discovering the real truth. 

What is worst in both of *Mr. Webb’s* works is that his representation of the actual monument in his drawings is fictitious. As *Mr. Aubrey* <note>John Aubrey (1626–1697), the first to scientifically survey Stonehenge</note> rightly observes, "in attempting to restore a piece of architecture according to *Vitruvius* <note>An ancient Roman architect whose rules for classical symmetry Webb tried to force onto the stones</note>, he deceives the reader with a false representation of the whole." It requires no great effort to prove this, nor do we need much time to be satisfied of it: the work still stands. As soon as a discerning eye visits the spot, we see that *Webb’s* equilateral triangles forming the inner cell are mere fancies; his three entrances across the ditch are also imaginary; and he has turned the cell sixty degrees from its true position to support his imaginary theory. But it is against my inclination to find fault with the work of others, nor do I seek to win over the reader to my side by doing so. I had great pleasure for several years in viewing and examining these noble remains of our ancestors. What I wrote about them was for my own private amusement and that of my friends. I publish them only for the honor of my country, and in hopes that such a work will be useful to religion—which is my ultimate goal.

Though *Stonehenge* is the most magnificent singularity of this sort in the world, as far as we know, there are many others clearly formed on the same or a similar design, by the same measurements, and for the same purpose, all over the *British* Isles. We have no reason to doubt they were made by the same people under the direction of the *British Druids*. There are countless examples, from Land’s End in *Cornwall* to the furthest northern promontory in *Scotland*, where *Roman* power never reached. They are found in all the islands between *Scotland* and *Ireland*, the *Isle of Man*, all the *Orkney* islands, etc., and are numerous in *Ireland* itself. There is no evidence, as far as I can see, for any other people or nations being their founders. 

They are circles of stones, generally unhewn, of different diameters, situated on elevated ground, barren heaths, and downs; mostly made of stones taken from the surface of the earth. There are no memories of the founders other than a continuous tradition that they are sacred; that they possess medicinal virtues; that they were made by the *Irish*; that they were brought from *Africa*; that they were "high places" of worship, sanctuaries, or places for bowing and adoration. The names they commonly have suggest the same thing. In many places, the specific memory and name of the *Druids* remain, and people bury their dead in or near them to this day, believing them to be holy ground. *Mr. Toland*, in his *History of the Druids* (page 23), tells us: "In *Gealcossa’s* mount <note>original: "Gealcossa" - meaning "White-legged"</note> in *Inishowen* in the county of *Donegal*, a Druidess of that name lived; it signifies 'white-legged,' according to the ancient fashion in..."

<meta>catchword: "Homer's"</meta>

# STONEHENGE

“... *Homer’s* time <note>Homer is the legendary Greek poet of the 8th century BCE; the author is establishing a very ancient timeline.</note>. On that hill is her grave and her temple, being a sort of diminutive *Stonehenge*, which the old *Irish*, at this day, dare not any way profane.” Many instances of this sort, of all these particulars, we have in our island: particularly the temple on *Temple-downs* by *Abury* <note>Now known as Avebury, a massive Neolithic henge monument in Wiltshire.</note>. Whatever is dug up in or near these works are manifestly remains of the Druid times; urns, bones, ornaments of amber, glass beads, snake-stones <note>Fossilized ammonites, which were often thought to be petrified snakes.</note>, amulets, celts <note>Prehistoric stone or bronze axe heads.</note>, flint-hatchets, arrow-heads, and such things as bespeak the rudest ages, the utmost antiquity, most early plantations of people that came into our island, soon after *Noah’s* flood. I have all the reason in the world to believe them an oriental colony of *Phœnicians*; at least that such a one came upon the first *Celtic* plantation of people here: which reasons will appear in the progress of this discourse. I suppose in matters of such extraordinary antiquity, it would be absurd to set about a formal demonstration; and those readers would be altogether unreasonable, that expect we prove every fact here, as they would do by living witnesses, before a court of judicature. When all is consider’d, that I have put together on this affair, a judicious person, I presume, will agree, I have made the matter sufficiently evident, and as much as the nature of things requires.

In the times just preceding the coming of the *Romans* into *Britain*, the *Belgæ* <note>A tribal confederation from northern Gaul (modern Belgium/France) who migrated to Britain.</note>, a most powerful colony from the *Gallic* continent, had firmly seated themselves all over the country, where *Stonehenge* is situate, quite to the southern sea; taking in the south part of *Wiltshire*, and all *Dorsetshire*. *Wiltshire* has its name from the river *Willy*, which in *Welsh* is *wyli*, in *Latin*, *to cry or wail* <note>original: *vagire*</note>, from its noise. A river of like name in *Northamptonshire*. Upon the former river at *Wilton*, probably liv’d the *Carvilius*, one of the four <margin>PLATE XXXIV.</margin> kings that fought *Julius Cæsar*, the picture of whose tumulus <note>A burial mound.</note> we have given towards the end. The *Belgæ* came into *Britain* upon the south, as other *Celtic* nations before had fix’d themselves from the east, *Kent*, the *Thames*, &c. such as the *Cantii*, *Segontiaci*, *Atrebates*, &c. so that in *Cæsar’s* time, all the south and east parts of *Britain* were dispossess’d of their original inhabitants, and peopled from the continent: and this very work of *Stonehenge* was in the hands of the *Belgæ*, who built it not. In my *curious itinerary* <note>Referring to the author’s own book, *Itinerarium Curiosum* (1724).</note>, p. 181. I observ’d no less than four successive boundary ditches here, from the southern shore; which with good reason, I suppos’d, were made by the *Belgæ*, as they conquer’d the country by degrees, from the aboriginal inhabitants. This shews, they must have been a long while about it, that the *Britons* disputed every inch of ground with them, and that for two reasons; as well because of the extraordinary beauty and goodness of the country, as fighting *for their altars and their homes* <note>original: *pro aris & focis*</note> for their great temple of *Stonehenge*: not to speak of that other greater temple, a little more northward, at *Abury*. The *Segontiaci* had got *Hampshire*, to the east of them, before, as far as the *Colinburn* river, and the *Atrebates*, *Berkshire*. The first ditch runs between the river of *Blandford*, formerly *Alauna*, and the river of *Bere*, the piddle in *Dorsetshire*, two or three miles south of it. The second runs to the north of *Cranborn* chase, upon the edge of *Wiltshire*, by *Pentridg*: it divides the counties of *Dorset* and *Wilts*. The third is conspicuous upon *Salisbury* plain, as we pass from *Wilton* to *Stonehenge*, about the two-mile stone, north of *Wilton*: it is drawn between the river *Avon* and the *Willy*, from *Dornford* to *Newton*. The fourth is the more famous *Wansdike*, of great extent. *Gwahan* in old *British* signifies *separation, distinction* <note>original: *separatio, distinctio*</note> *guahanu* to separate <note>original: *guahanu seperare*</note>, and that undoubtedly gave name to the ditch. The method of all these ditches, is, to take the northern edge of a ridge of hills, which is always steep; the bank is on the south side. And in my *itinerary*, p. 134. I shew’d a most evident demonstration, that it was <margin>PLATE II.</margin> made before the time of the *Romans*, in the passage of the *Roman* road down *Runway* hill. PLATE II. *Wansdike* is the last advanc’d post of the *Belgæ* northwards, and that it was made after *Stonehenge* was built, is plain, because the stones that compose the work, were brought from *Marlborough* <meta>The text continues onto the next page with: downs</meta>

# STONEHENGE #

> " … *Homer's* time. On that hill is her grave and her temple, being a sort of
> " diminutive *Stonehenge*, which the old *Irish*, at this day, dare not any way
> " profane." 

We find many examples of this kind throughout our island, particularly the temple on the *Temple-downs* near *Abury* <note>Modern Avebury, a massive Neolithic henge monument in Wiltshire.</note>. Whatever is excavated within or near these works clearly belongs to the era of the Druids; these finds include urns, bones, amber ornaments, glass beads, snake-stones <note>Fossilized ammonites or ancient glass beads, often believed in folklore to have been created by magical serpents.</note>, amulets, celts <note>Prehistoric stone or bronze axe-like tools.</note>, flint-hatchets, arrow-heads, and other objects that suggest the most primitive ages and the greatest antiquity—the very earliest settlements of people who arrived on our island shortly after *Noah’s* flood. I have every reason to believe they were an eastern colony of *Phoenicians* <note>A seafaring civilization from the Levant. Eighteenth-century antiquarians often theorized that the Phoenicians traded with Britain for tin and brought middle-eastern culture with them.</note>; or at least, that such a group arrived during the first *Celtic* settlement here. The reasons for this will become clear as this discourse progresses. I suppose that in matters of such extreme antiquity, it would be foolish to attempt a formal, legal proof; it would be unreasonable for readers to expect every fact to be proven by living witnesses as if in a court of law. When all the evidence I have gathered is considered together, I believe a discerning person will agree that I have made the matter sufficiently clear, as much as the nature of ancient history allows.

<margin>TAB. XXXIV.</margin>
<margin>TAB. II.</margin>
In the period just before the *Romans* arrived in *Britain*, the *Belgae* <note>A group of tribes from northern Gaul (modern Belgium/France) who migrated to southern Britain.</note>—a powerful colony from the *Gallic* continent—had firmly established themselves throughout the region where *Stonehenge* stands, reaching all the way to the southern sea and occupying southern *Wiltshire* and all of *Dorsetshire*. *Wiltshire* takes its name from the river *Willy* <note>The River Wylye.</note>, which in *Welsh* is *wyli*, and in *Latin* is *vagire* <note>original: "vagire", meaning to cry out or wail.</note>, named for its noise. There is a river with a similar name in *Northamptonshire*. Near the former river at *Wilton*, there likely lived *Carvilius*, one of the four kings who fought against *Julius Caesar*; we have provided an illustration of his *tumulus* <note>A burial mound.</note> toward the end of this book. The *Belgae* entered *Britain* from the south, just as other *Celtic* nations before them had settled from the east—in *Kent*, along the *Thames*, and so on—such as the *Cantii*, *Segontiaci*, and *Atrebates*. Consequently, by *Caesar’s* time, the original inhabitants of the southern and eastern parts of *Britain* had been displaced and the land repopulated from the continent. Thus, *Stonehenge* was in the hands of the *Belgae*, though they were not the ones who built it. In my *Itinerarium Curiosum* <note>original: "itinerarium curiosum", referring to Stukeley's earlier book, "A Journey of Curiosities."</note> (p. 181), I noted at least four successive boundary ditches in this area, stretching from the southern shore. I have good reason to suppose these were made by the *Belgae* as they gradually conquered the country from the original inhabitants. This shows that the process must have taken a long time, and that the *Britons* disputed every inch of ground with them for two reasons: the extraordinary beauty and fertility of the land, and the defense of their great temple of *Stonehenge*—fighting *for their altars and their hearths* <note>original: "pro aris & focis", a Latin idiom meaning to fight for one's home, religion, and way of life.</note>. This is to say nothing of that other, even larger temple slightly further north at *Abury*. The *Segontiaci* had previously taken *Hampshire* to the east as far as the *Colinburn* river, while the *Atrebates* held *Berkshire*. The first ditch runs between the river of *Blandford* (formerly *Alauna*) and the river *Bere* (the Piddle) in *Dorsetshire*, a few miles south of it. The second runs north of *Cranborn* chase, along the edge of *Wiltshire* by *Pentridge*; it marks the border between the counties of *Dorset* and *Wilts*. The third is clearly visible on *Salisbury* plain as one travels from *Wilton* to *Stonehenge*, near the two-mile stone north of *Wilton*; it is drawn between the rivers *Avon* and *Willy*, from *Dornford* to *Newton*. The fourth is the more famous *Wansdike* <note>Wansdyke, a long medieval or late-antique earthwork.</note>, which is of great extent. The word *Gwahan* in old *British* signifies *separation* or *distinction* <note>original: "separatio, distinctio guahanu seperare"</note>, and that undoubtedly gave the ditch its name. The design of all these ditches is to follow the northern edge of a ridge of hills, which is always steep; the bank is on the south side. In my itinerary (p. 134), I provided clear proof that these were made before the *Roman* era, based on how the *Roman* road descends *Runway hill*. (See TABLE II). *Wansdike* represents the last advanced northern post of the *Belgae*, and it is plain it was made after *Stonehenge* was already built, because the stones used in that monument were brought from the *Marlborough*
downs

### DESCRIBED.

downs in north *Wiltshire*, beyond the dike; and as then in an enemy’s country. And most probably it was built before the *Belgae* <note>An Iron Age tribal confederation from northern Gaul that migrated to Britain around 100 BCE.</note> set footing in *Britain*, because of the great number of barrows or sepulchral *tumuli* <term>tumuli: ancient burial mounds or heaps of earth and stones</term> about it, which, no doubt, were made for the burial of kings and great men.

The stones of which *Stonehenge* is composed, beyond any controversy, came from those called the gray weathers <note>Large blocks of sarsen stone (silicified sandstone) found naturally on the Marlborough Downs.</note>, upon *Marlborough* downs near *Abury* <note>The modern village and site of Avebury.</note>; where is that other most wonderful work of this sort, which I shall describe in my next volume. This is 15 or 16 miles off. All the greater stones are of that sort, except the altar, which is of a still harder, as designed to resist fire. The pyramidals <note>Referring to the smaller stones, known today as bluestones.</note> likewise are of a different sort, and much harder than the rest, like those of that other Druid temple called the *Weddings*, at *Stanton-Drew* in *Somersetshire*. Dr. *Halley* <note>Edmond Halley (1656–1742), the famous astronomer and mathematician.</note> was at *Stonehenge* in the year 1720, and brought a piece of it to the Royal Society. I examined it with a microscope. It is a composition of crystals of red, green and white colors, cemented together by nature’s art, with opaque granules of flinty or stony matter. The Doctor observed from the general wear of the weather upon the stones, that the work must be of an extraordinary antiquity, and for all he knew, 2 or 3000 years old. But had the Doctor been at *Abury*, which is made of the same stones, he might well from the like argumentation conclude, that work as old again as *Stonehenge*, at least much older, and I verily believe it. Nevertheless the current of so many ages has been more merciful to *Stonehenge*, than the insolence of rapacious hands, (besides the general looting brought upon the work of old) by the unaccountable folly of mankind, in breaking pieces off with great hammers. This detestable practice arose from the silly notion of the stones being factitious <term>factitious: artificial or man-made; a common 18th-century theory suggested the stones were cast from a type of ancient concrete</term>. But, alas! it would be a greater wonder to make them by art, than to carry them 16 miles by art and strength; and those people must be inexcusable, that deface the monument for so trifling a fancy. Another argument of vulgar thoughtlessness, is, that all the wonder of the work consists, in the difficulty of counting the stones; and with that, the infinite numbers of daily visitors busy themselves. This seems to be the remains of superstition, and the notion of magic, not yet got out of people's heads, since Druid-times. But indeed a serious view of this magnificent wonder, is apt to put a thinking and judicious person into a kind of ecstasy, when he views the struggle between art and nature, the grandeur of that art that hides itself, and seems unartful. For though the contrivance that put this massive frame together, must have been exquisite, yet the founders endeavored to hide it, by the seeming rudeness of the work. The bulk of the constituent parts is so very great, that the mortises and tenons <term>mortises and tenons: a jointing technique where a projecting "tongue" (tenon) fits into a corresponding hole (mortise)</term> must have been prepared to an extreme nicety, and, like the fabric of *Solomon’s* temple, every stone tallied; and neither axes nor hammers were heard upon the whole structure <note>A reference to the biblical description in 1 Kings 6:7, where the temple was built of stones finished at the quarry so no tool noise was heard at the site.</note>. Nevertheless there is not a stone at *Stonehenge*, that felt not, more or less, both axe and hammer of the founders. Yet it is highly entertaining to consider the judicious carelessness therein, really the "grand gusto" <note>original: "grand gusto"; an 18th-century term for a high, noble, or masterful artistic style</note>, like a great master in drawing, secure of the effect: a true masterpiece. Everything proper, bold, astonishing. The lights and shades adapted with inconceivable justness. Notwithstanding the monstrous size of the work, and every part of it; it is far from appearing heavy: it is composed of several species of work, and the proportions of the dissimilar parts recommend the whole, and it pleases like a magical spell. No one thinks any part of it too great or too little, too high or too low. And we that can only view it in its ruins, the less regret those ruins, that, if possible, add to its solemn majesty.

The stones of the gray weathers are of a "bastard" <note>inferior or irregular</note> sort of white marble, and lie upon the surface of the ground, in infinite numbers, and of all dimensions. They are loose, detached from any rock, and doubtless lay there ever since the creation. Being solid parts thrown out to the surface

### STONEHENGE

of the fluid globe <note>The author refers to the geological theory that the earth was once a liquid mass and the stones formed as it cooled and rotated.</note>, when its rotation was first impressed. All our Druid temples are built where these sort of stones from the surface can be had at reasonable distances; for they are never taken from quarries. There is a very good quarry at *Chilmark* in this country. *Salisbury* cathedral, and all the great buildings are from there; but it is a stone quite different to our work. It was a matter of much labour to draw them hither, 16 miles. My friend the reverend Dr. *Stephen Hales* <note>Stephen Hales (1677–1761) was a famous clergyman and scientist who made major contributions to botany and physiology.</note>, the excellent author of *Vegetable Statics*, and other works, computed them as follows. The stone at the upper end of the cell, which is fallen down and broke in half, is in length (says he) 25 feet, in breadth 7 feet, and in thickness at a medium 3½, amounts to 612 cubic feet. Now a cubic foot of *Hedington* stone weighs near 154¼ pounds troy <term>troy pounds: a system of weight often used for precious metals, where one pound is 12 ounces</term>. If *Stonehenge* stone be of the same specific gravity <term>specific gravity: the density of a substance compared to water</term>, it will amount to 94,348 pounds, which is 31½ tuns <note>An old spelling of "tons."</note>. But if this be of the same specific gravity as *Burford* stone, which weighs 155¾ the cubic foot, then it will weigh 95,319 pounds troy, or 32 tuns. If it be equal to *Blaidon* stone, which is 187 pounds troy per cubic foot, then it weighs 114,444 pounds troy, or 38 tuns. But I am sure that the stone is of considerably larger dimensions than what Dr. *Hales* has stated it at, and that the sort of stone is much heavier than that of the largest specific gravity he speaks of, and that it amounts to more than 40 tuns, and requires more than 140 oxen to draw it; yet this is not the heaviest stone at the place.

The notion we ought to entertain of *Stonehenge* is not a little enhanced by the discovery I made from frequent measurements there. It gave me the opportunity of finding out the standard and original measure, which the people used who made this and all other works of this kind. And this precludes any tedious disputation against the opinion of authors; for whoever makes any eminent building, most certainly forms it upon the common measure in use among the people of that place. Therefore if the proportions of *Stonehenge* fall into fractions and uncouth numbers when measured by the *English*, *French*, *Roman*, or *Grecian* foot, we may assuredly conclude the architects were neither *English*, *French*, *Roman*, or *Greeks*. Thus, for instance, when the accurate *Greaves* <note>John Greaves (1602–1652), an English mathematician and antiquary who measured the Pyramids and Roman ruins.</note> tells us the door of the *Pantheon* (which is of one stone) is of *English* foot-measure 19 foot <gloss>602/1000</gloss> within: should we not be apt to assert at first sight that the architect in so costly a work did not choose his measures at random, but intended that this dimension should be 20 feet? When we consider this building is at *Rome*, and that it amounts to 20 *Roman* feet, must we not conclude it was erected by the *Roman* standard? adding too, that all the rest of the dimensions of this stately structure fall aptly and judiciously into the same scale. So as long as any traces <note>original: "vestigia"</note> of *St. Paul's* cathedral remain, the *English* foot, by which it was built, will easily be known. I must prepare the reader for a right understanding of our *Druid* edifices by informing him that *Stonehenge*, and all other works of this nature in our island, are erected by that most ancient measure called a *cubit*, which we read of in the holy scriptures, and in ancient profane <note>In this context, "profane" simply means secular or non-religious ancient authors.</note> authors. I mean the same individual measure called the *Hebrew*, *Egyptian*, *Phoenician* cubit; most probably derived from *Noah* and *Adam*. It is the same that the pyramids of *Egypt* and other their works are projected upon; the same as that of *Moses*'s tabernacle, *Solomon's* temple, etc. and we may reasonably pride ourselves in possessing these visible monuments of the old measure of the world. My predecessor Bishop *Cumberland* shows, enough to satisfy us, that the *Egyptian* and *Hebrew* measure was the same, though he has not hit upon that measure to a nicety. My friend and colleague Dr. *Arbuthnot* has been more successful in applying it to such parts of the greater pyramid as evidently establish its proportion to our *English* foot, from the measures *Greaves* has left us: and shows it to be 20 inches and ¼ of *English* measure. Thus the Doctor observes the side of the greater pyramid at base is 693 *English* feet; which

### DESCRIBED.

which amounts exactly to 400 *Egyptian* cubits, a full and suitable number for such a square work, and without question the originally designed measure, the <term>stadium<note>An ancient Greek unit of distance, usually about 600 feet.</note></term> of old. I have taken notice that *Inigo Jones* <note>A famous 17th-century architect who argued that Stonehenge was a Roman temple.</note> observed the like dimensions in laying out the plot of *Lincoln’s-Inn-fields* <note>A large public square in London; the author is comparing its size to the base of the Great Pyramid.</note>. The Doctor adds many more instances, deduced in the same way, to confirm it. I add, that *Greaves* <note>John Greaves, a mathematician who measured the pyramids in the 1630s.</note> says, the lowermost steps of the pyramid are near 4 feet in height, which amounts to 2 cubits and 4 palms. They are 3 foot in breadth, i.e. 1 cubit 4 palms. The length of the declining first entrance is 92 feet and an half, i.e. 55 cubits. The length of the next gallery is 110 feet, which amounts to 60 cubits. There is another gallery in the pyramid, of the same length. Mr. *Webb* <note>John Webb, an architect and son-in-law to Inigo Jones.</note> says the diameter of *Stonehenge* is 110 feet. This would tempt one to suspect the same measure used in both. Thus the diameter of the like work at *Rollright* in *Oxfordshire*, described by Dr. *Plot*, is 35 yards, i.e. 110 feet grossly measured. Father *Brothais* in his observations on upper *Egypt*, in our *Philosophical Transactions* <note>The scientific journal of the Royal Society.</note> found a door-case made of one stone, in a magnificent building, it was 26 ½ feet in height, this is 15 cubits. Dr. *Huntington* in the same *Transactions* says, he found the sphinx standing by the northern pyramid to be 110 feet in circuit, i.e. 60 cubits. *Ptolemy* in his IVth book, and *Philosophical Transactions* XXXVI.—speak of the obelisk raised by king *Rameses* at *Heliopolis*, which Mr. *Webb*, p. 34. gives the length in *English* feet, 136. This is 80 cubits. That which *Augustus* set up in the *Great Circus* <note>original: "circus maximus"</note> at *Rome* upon his reduction of *Egypt*, *Webb* says, is 120 feet 9 inches, which amounts to 70 cubits. Another, *Augustus* set up in the *Field of Mars* <note>original: "campus martius"</note>, which he says is 9 foot higher, i.e. 5 cubits. He speaks again of that erected by *Fontana* before St. *Peter’s* 81 feet, which was 50 cubits. I suppose the base being injured, it was cut a little shorter. This at the base, he says, is 9 foot square, i.e. 5 cubits. The *Vatican* obelisk is 170 foot high, which is 100 cubits. 12 foot broad at bottom, which is 7 cubits; at top a third part less.

Hence we gather, the measure of the show-bread table of the *Jews*, a cubit and half in height, *Exodus 25:23*. It had a golden crown about it, meaning a moulding, or verge or cornice, as upon our tea-tables. A periphery or crown <note>original Hebrew/Greek/Latin: "ויר פריפריא, corna" [likely referring to a "rim" or "border"].</note>, because 12 loaves were to be piled upon it. It was 31 inches in height, that of our ordinary eating-tables. And we shall find by this same cubit divided into its 6 <term>tophachs<note>Hebrew "tefakh," meaning a handbreadth or palm.</note></term> or palms, all our *Druid* works are performed. It is not to be wondered at, that by this same *oriental* cubit, an eastern colony under the conduct of the *Egyptian, Tyrian, Phoenician Hercules*, (who was the same person) about *Abraham’s* time, or soon after, as I have good reason to believe, which will be shown in its proper place.

### CHAP. II.

*Of the name of Stonehenge*. *These works prior to the Roman times*. *Who were the builders*? *Of the general situation of it, again*. *Of the beauty of its general proportion*. *A peep into it*. *A walk round the area*. *Remarks on two stones standing on the embankment, and two corresponding cavities for water vases: explained from ancient coins*. *That the Welsh are the remains of the Belgae from the continent, who lived here at the Roman invasion, and by whose reports, Stonehenge was built by the most ancient oriental colony, that brought the Druids hither*.

...have called it by a more honourable name. <term>Rose hengenne</term> <note>Likely a variation of the Old English *Stan-hengen*.</note> is in <term>Saxon</term> <note>The language of the Anglo-Saxons, spoken in England from the 5th to the 11th centuries.</note> a hanging-rod or pole, *i. e.* a gallows; and <term>Stonehenge</term> is a stone gallows, called so from the hanging parts, architraves <note>The horizontal beams that rest on top of the vertical columns.</note>, or rather imposts, the more remarkable part, and which only can persuade people from thinking, the stones grew in the very place, (as they express it.) And so <term>Mr. Camden</term>, <term>Dr. Holland</term>, <term>Mr. Webb</term> and others think, of the wonderful work at <term>Abury</term> <note>Now known as Avebury, a massive Neolithic henge monument in Wiltshire.</note>; because there are none of these overthwart stones, as here. Many are astonish'd at the bulk of these stones, that measuring all art and power by their own, they had rather think, they sprouted up in their places, like mushrooms, at regular distances, in mathematical circles; than that they were plac'd there by human industry, for excellent purpose. But pendulous rocks are now called <term>henges</term> in <term>Yorkshire</term>, and I have been informed of another place there called <term>Stonehenge</term>, being natural rocks. So that I doubt not, <term>Stonehenge</term> in <term>Saxon</term> signifies the hanging stones. In <term>Cornwall</term> is a <term>Heath</term> call'd now <term>Hengston</term> down, probably from such a work as ours, now demolished. It is in the hundred of <term>Easte</term>. And near it, is that other memorable Antiquity, composed of many upright stones, call'd the <term>Hurlers</term>, a <term>Druid</term> temple. The old <term>Britons</term> or <term>Welsh</term> call <term>Stonehenge chor gaur</term>, which some interpret <note>original: "chorea gigantum"</note> the giants' dance: I judge, more rightly <note>original: "chorus magnus"</note> the great choir, round church, or temple. As <term>Banchor</term> <note>Modern Bangor; the author suggests the name implies a significant religious site.</note> (where probably was of old, another <term>Druid temple</term>) means the high temple. But they mistake it for <note>original: "chorea, chwarae xuare"</note> a ball or dance; as <term>Necham</term> <note>Alexander Neckam, a 12th-century English scholar.</note> sings;

->Noble is the structure of stones, the dance of giants:<-
->Art, having tested its power, has finished the work.<-
<note>original Latin: "Nobilis est lapidum structura, chorea gigantum: Ars experta suum posse, peregit opus."</note>

<term>Mr. Camden</term> defines the work <note>original: "corona in modum"</note> in the manner of a crown. The <term>Latin corona</term> a crown, <term>corolla</term> a ghirland <note>garland</note>, and the <term>British crown</term> comes from its circular form, as <term>côr</term> <note>original: "chorus"</note> choir. The Armorican <term>Britons</term> call <term>cryn</term> <note>original: "rotundus"</note> round, as do <term>kruin</term> the <term>Irish</term>. <term>Coryn</term> is the round tip of any thing, many such like words in all the <term>Celtic</term> dialects. The chorus of a building among <term>Roman Christians</term>, became appropriate to the more sacred part, or east end of churches, always turn'd of a circular form; from the time of <term>Constantine the Great</term>. Thus all the churches in the holy land, thus the chapel in <term>Colchester</term> castle, and in the <term>Tower of London</term>, (both, in my opinion, built about his time) are round at the east end. The old <term>Britons</term> or <term>Welsh</term>, we find, had a notion of its being a sacred place, tho’ they were not the builders of it; for I take them to be the remains of the <term>Celtic</term> people that came from the continent, who chiefly inhabited <term>England</term>, at least the south part, when the <term>Romans</term> invaded the island, they are more particularly the remains of the <term>Belgæ</term>. I suppose their name <term>Welsh</term>, a corruption of <term>Belgæ</term>, <gloss>Ouelgai</gloss> <note>original Greek: "Οὐέλγαι"</note> in Greek, *<term>Belgischen</term>* and *<term>Welschen</term>* in German. <term>Strabo IV.</term> speaks of their way of making flannel, called <gloss>lainai</gloss> <note>original Greek: "λαιναι"</note>, for which our <term>Irish</term> are so famous. <term>Strabo</term> gives the <term>Celtic word</term> without the guttural aspirate, *<term>chlæna</term>* in Latin. The most ancient inhabitants, the remains of the old <term>Phoenician colony</term> and primitive <term>Celts</term> who built <term>Stonehenge</term>, were the <term>Picts</term>, <term>Scots</term>, <term>Highland</term> and <term>Irish</term>, all the same people, tho’ perhaps differing somewhat in dialect, as in situation: no otherwise than a <term>Cumberland</term>-man and one of <term>Somersetshire</term> now. The <term>Cornish</term>, I suppose, some remains too, of the old oriental race. But at this very day in <term>Wales</term>, they call every antiquated appearance beyond memory, <term>Irish</term>. Upon view of land, that from before any ones remembrance appears to have been plow'd, or very ancient ruins of buildings, and the like, they immediately pronounce, That it was in the times of the <term>Irish</term>. The very same is observable in the north of the <term>Picts</term> or <term>Pights</term>, as they pronounce it, gutturally, in the oriental fashion, which we cannot imitate. They call old foundations, <term>Pights</term> houses, &c. Every thing is <term>Pictish</term>, whose origin they do not know. These people are conscious, that they are not the <term>Aborigines</term> <note>The original inhabitants who were there from the beginning.</note>, who by time and...

# STONEHENGE

<margin>Plate IV.</margin>
made for use and instruction, like mathematical figures, and cannot be expected much to please the eye; being formed chiefly from bare lines, admitting no picture-like decoration.

I have observed another similar proof of these works being older than the *Roman* times here, in that *Roman* road that goes from *Marlborough* to *Bath*. It is near *Abury* <note>Avebury</note>, and I have a print of it engraved, which will be exhibited, when I next publish an account of that great work. But in the former plate IV. I call those *Druid* barrows <term>barrow: an ancient burial mound</term>, which are often found on these plains: a circular trench, sometime of 100 foot diameter, with only a small tump <note>a small mound or hillock</note> of earth in the middle, under which there is commonly an urn. Sometime two or three of these little tumps or diminutive *burial mounds* <note>original Latin: "tumuli"</note> within one circle, which it is natural to suppose, were friends or relations. These circles are always excellently well marked out.

The particular spot of ground where *Stonehenge* stands, is in the lordship of west or little *Ambrefbury* <note>Amesbury</note>: the possession of the reverend Mr. *Hayward*, who at present may be called the *Archdruid* of the island. 'Tis a delicate part of this large plain, with a gentle declivity from the south-west to the south and north-east. So that the soil, which is chalk, is perfectly dry and hard. Hence the infinite numbers of coaches and horses, that through so many centuries have been visiting the place every day, have not obliterated the track of the banks and ditches. The water cannot possibly rest any where hereabouts. The founders consulted well for the stability of their work, and salubrity <note>healthfulness or pleasantness</note> of the place. *Caesar* informs us in his commentaries <note>Julius Caesar’s "Commentaries on the Gallic War," Book 6, Chapter 13</note>, that among the *Druids*, “one has the supreme authority. When he is dead, whoever excels in dignity succeeds. But if there be more candidates, the *Archdruid* is chose by the votes of the *Druids*: and sometimes they fight for it. At a certain fixed time of the year the *Gaulish Druids* meet, in the territories of the *Carnutes* <note>A Celtic tribe in central France</note>, which country is in the middle of *Gaul*, in a consecrated place. Hither all persons from all quarters come, who have any controversy, and stand to their determination. The discipline of the *Druids* arose in *Britain*, and is said from thence to have been brought into *Gaul*. And now, they who design to be more thoroughly initiated therein, go over to learn.” Here in few lines the great author acquaints us with a vast fund of ancient history, and upon which whole volumes have been wrote. I observe no more from it at present, than that we may very reasonably conclude, the elegant and the magnificent structure of *Stonehenge* was as the metropolitical <note>The primary or "cathedral" church of a region</note> church of the chief *Druid* of *Britain*. This was the *consecrated place* <note>original Latin: "locus consecratus"</note> where they met at some great festivals in the year, as well to perform the extraordinary sacrifices and religious rites, as to determine causes and civil matters. *Caesar* calls these appointments of the *Druids* in *Gaul* consecrated places, where probably was nothing but a circle of rude stones. Had he seen those of our island, an *Abury* or even a *Stonehenge*, he would scarce have given them the title of temples: he was not used to the old patriarchal way. But I reckon the true reading in that passage quoted from him, to be *in a consecrated place* <note>original Latin: "loco consecrato"</note>, not *in a grove* <note>original Latin: "luco"</note>, which was put in by some bold transcriber, who had heard of the fondness of the *Druids* for groves. But how unfit is a grove for a great and public meeting upon civil affairs? And this for the excellency of its situation upon a vast plain, was well calculated for a public meeting of those of the order, at an election of a new *Archdruid*. As *Caesar*'s words give light to the work before us, so it confirms what the warlike author says, of the discipline being originally in *Britain*; which the critics upon the continent cannot bear, and vainly endeavour to spirit away *Caesar*'s meaning. The very building of *Stonehenge*, to say nothing of other like works here, shows it was not in vain, that the youth of *Gaul* came to learn of men, who could contrive and execute so mighty a work.

Stonehenge stands not upon the very summit of a hill, but pretty near it,

## DESCRIBED.

<margin>PLATE V.</margin>
The monument stands on a slight elevation, and for more than three-quarters of the surrounding circle, you ascend to it very gently from the lower ground. At a distance of half a mile, its appearance is stately and awe-inspiring—truly majestic. As you move closer, especially when walking up the avenue to the north-east (the side that remains most complete today), the sheer scale of its outline fills the eye in an astonishing manner. The most impressive view is the front prospect seen from the entrance of the avenue. The structure that leans over the high altar is visible through the grand or principal entrance because the observer stands on lower ground. If the reader wishes to look at *Plate XII*, it is represented there in <term>orthography: a technical architectural drawing showing a perfectly flat front view</term>, just as it appears here in person. By using this method of comparing the drawings together, we may, without confusion, gain a true understanding of the work. *Stonehenge* is significantly larger in diameter than the exterior of the dome of *St. Paul’s* Cathedral in London. From a comparison of these two buildings, I was able to judge the vanity of the architect of *St. Peter’s* in *Rome* <note>The author likely refers to Bramante or Michelangelo</note>, who, in an attempt to outdo the *Pantheon* while imitating it, boasted that he would "set the *Pantheon* 200 feet high in the air," referring to the massive dome he was building. However, the architect of the *Pantheon*, *Valerius Ostiensis* <note>An ancient Roman architect mentioned in historical records</note>, had he been alive, would have told him that the vastness of the diameter in these domes is lost when they are placed at such a great height. Whatever we wish to be admired should be kept as the largest visible dimension. Therefore, *Valerius*, with admirable judgment, made the outward breadth of the *Pantheon* a full fifth part wider than its height when viewed from the front; but if we measure it from the side, including the porch, the breadth is to the height as more than 6 to 4. By this design, the wonder of the *Pantheon*—the great curve or arch 150 *Roman* feet in diameter—remains visible. Similarly, the curve of *Stonehenge*, which is over 100 *English* feet, appears extraordinarily large and well-proportioned, reaching to the top of the outer <term>cornice: the decorative molding at the top of a wall or structure</term>; the inner cornices, on average, are only 24 feet high. For the cornices of the inner part of *Stonehenge*—what the architect *Webb* <note>John Webb (1611–1672), who argued Stonehenge was a Roman temple</note> calls the "cell"—are not all of equal height, a detail I shall discuss in the proper place. Thus, both elements of the wonder are preserved: the greatness of the entire circuit and the impressive height of the individual parts, with the height being one-fourth of the total diameter. The dramatic play of light and shadow at *Stonehenge*, as well as the variety created by its circular form, gives it every possible visual advantage, making it truly deserve the title of:

->The glorious house of the Gods,<-
<note>original Latin: "Deorum gloriosa domus"</note>

<margin>PLATE XII.</margin>
as the ancient writers *Theocritus* and *Herodotus* generally call temples. Its situation also matches ancient ideals. The historian *Pausanias* praises the *Tanagrei* people in *Boeotia* <note>A region in ancient Greece</note> for placing their temples in clean, distinct areas, far removed from common buildings and the noise of trade.

<margin>PLATE XXIII.</margin>
*Stonehenge* is enclosed within a circular ditch. Once a person has crossed this ditch, says the Right Reverend annotator to *Camden* <note>This refers to Bishop Edmund Gibson, who updated William Camden’s famous survey of Britain</note>, he walks 35 yards before reaching the stone structure itself. This measurement is the same as the 110 feet mentioned by *Webb* as the diameter of the work. The area enclosed by the ditch, where *Stonehenge* sits, has a diameter three times that of the stone circle itself. See the *Plate* showing the area. Therefore, the distance from the inner edge of the ditch to the stones, all the way around, is equal to the diameter of the Temple itself. The reader will recall my promise regarding the scale or measurement used by the *Druids* to build this and all their other works: it is the ancient *Hebrew*, *Phoenician*, or *Egyptian* Cubit. When compared to the *English* foot, this cubit measures 20 and 4/5 inches. Therefore, I have prepared the following comparison between our *English* and the *Hebrew* scale, which will guide us through the rest of this description. To ensure I am not suspected of manipulating the data to fit my own theory, I will use the measurements recorded by others whenever they are available in print, provided...

<margin>PLATE VI.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the author’s architectural study of Stonehenge, shifting from a general description of the site’s majesty to the technical challenges of measuring it. He introduces his theory of the "Druid cubit," drawing parallels between the measurements used by the ancient builders of Stonehenge and those mentioned in the Bible and classical Roman texts.</meta>

### STONEHENGE

...provided they are done with reasonable judgment and accuracy; for both are necessary in our case, with proper allowance for the circumstances. It is not to be supposed that in this work, the extreme detail and meticulousness of *Desgodetz* <note>Antoine Desgodetz was a French architect famous for his 1682 publication containing highly precise measurements of ancient Roman ruins.</note>, with which he measured the remains of old *Rome*, is expected, or even possible. For though the stones are not chiseled and squared with such precision as *Roman* works are, they are indeed chiseled, and are far from unrefined. Nevertheless, not everyone has the skill to properly measure them. For they are much worn by the weather, and much has been knocked off by the hands of vandals. Those stones that still stand are shifted in various ways by time and their own weight; by foolish people digging around them, and by the unfortunate colony of rabbits lately moved there. So that we may well say with the poet *Claudian*,

->*Ready to fall in a long-delayed ruin from its own leaning weight,*<-
->*part collapses from the constant wind, part, worn away by rain,*<-
->*breaks; decaying old age has torn a piece away.*<-
<note>original: "Seram ponderibus pronis tractura ruinam, / Pars cadit assiduo flatu, pars imbre peresa / Rumpitur, abrupuit partem vitiosa vetustas."</note>

<margin>PLATE VI.</margin>
I was forced to make many repeated measurements before I could obtain an exact ground-plan; it required much consideration to find the true scale by which it was composed: the <term>Druid cubit: a unit of measurement which the author believes the builders brought with them from the East</term>. Therefore, by the attached scales in <term>PLATE VI.</term>, which I have designed to account for all lengths, the reader will most perfectly understand the following description and see the truth of my claim. From there, one may be enabled to measure any other similar works in our islands which I have not had the opportunity to view. It was the Eastern way, when laying out a building, to use a <term>staff: a measuring rod</term> of 6 <term>cubits: an ancient unit of length based on the forearm</term> long. This was a convenient, manageable length, and its division into half a dozen parts suited a system of counting by <term>duodenaries: units of twelve</term>. Thus in *Ezekiel* 40:3, 5 and *Revelation* 21:16, the angel who laid out the temple of <term>Solomon</term> is described as having a <term>measuring reed of 6 cubits</term> (a measuring rod or cane) in his hand. This being the universal and first measure of antiquity, it was in time spread all over the world. In particular, it became the <term>decempedum: a ten-foot measuring rod</term> of the *Greeks* and *Romans*—their common measuring standard. But it is remarkable that they altered the divisions, thinking it more skillful and convenient to have them in smaller parts; instead of 6 cubits, they made it consist of 10 feet. And by time and change, the whole measure became somewhat altered from the original. For the *Greek* ten-foot rod grew somewhat too long, just as the *Romans* shortened theirs a little. *Ezekiel's* reed is our 10 feet and 4 and 2/3 inches; 400 cubits is the <term>stadium: an ancient Greek unit of distance</term> of the ancients, or a <term>furlong</term> of 700 feet.

<margin>PLATE VII.</margin>
When you enter the building, whether on foot or horseback, and cast your eyes around upon the gaping ruins, you are struck into a state of wonder and joy which none can describe, and which only those who feel it can understand. Other buildings fall piece by piece, but here a single stone is a ruin in itself, lying like the haughty carcass of the giant *Goliath*. Yet there is enough of it left standing to enable us to reconstruct its form as it was in its most perfect state. There is enough of every part to preserve the idea of the whole. The next illustration, <term>PLATE VII.</term>, the "peep" (as I call it) <note>The author uses "peep" to describe a specific interior perspective or view.</note> into the <term>sanctum sanctorum: the "Holy of Holies" or the innermost sacred chamber</term>, is drawn from the very entrance, looking into the inside. When we advance further, the dark portion of the heavy <term>imposts: horizontal stones resting on top of the pillars</term> over our heads, the gap of sky between the <term>jambs: vertical side-posts</term> of the inner <term>cell: the central room of the temple</term>, the strange construction of the whole, and the greatness of every part, is surprising. We may well cry out in the poet's words:

->*To such heights of power could religion reach!*<-
<note>original: "Tantum Relligio potuit!" — a famous line from Lucretius often used to describe the immense influence or awe-inspiring nature of religious works.</note>

If you look upon the parts that remain perfect, you imagine entire quarries have been lifted into the air; if you look upon the rough wreckage below, you see, as it were, the bowels...

The <term>vallum<note>A Latin term for an earthwork wall or rampart, usually accompanied by a ditch.</note></term> of the ditch which incloses the <term>area<note>The central open space of the monument.</note></term>, or court, is inwards, and makes a circular terrace; walking upon which, we take the foregoing prospects. The lowest part of the *area* is towards the entrance. The tops of all the circumjacent hills, or rather easy elevations, are covered over, as it were, with <term>barrows<note>Ancient burial mounds made of earth and stones.</note></term>, which cause an agreeable appearance; adorning the bare downs with their figures. And this ring of barrows reaches no further than until you lose sight of the temple, or thereabouts. Stand at the grand entrance by the stone that lies upon the ground, and the view of the temple presents itself as in the 5th *Plate*, the front prospect of *Stonehenge*.
<margin>Table V.</margin>
Directly down the avenue, to the north-east, the apex of a hill terminates the horizon, between which and the bottom of a valley you see the <term>Cursus<note>A massive, elongated Neolithic earthwork. Stukeley was the first to identify and name this feature, believing it to be a Roman-style racing track.</note></term>, a work which has never yet been taken notice of. It is a space of ground included between two long banks going parallel east and west, at 350 foot distance, the length 10,000 feet. This was designed for the horse races and games, like the *Olympic*, the *Isthmian*, etc., of the *Greeks*. But we shall speak more particularly of this afterwards. In the valley on this side of it, the straight part of the avenue terminates in two branches; that on the left hand leads to the *Cursus*; that on the right goes directly up the hill, between two famous groups of barrows, each consisting of seven in number. The farthest, or those northward, I call the oldest king’s barrows; the hithermost are vulgarly called the seven kings’ graves.

<margin>Table VIII.</margin>
If we walk a little to the left hand, Table VIII. is presented. See the northern long barrow: on this side of which, the eye takes in the whole length of the *Cursus*. Many barrows at the end and on both sides of it. That marked P. was opened by my Lord *Pembroke*, those marked S. were opened by myself. What was discovered therein will be treated of hereafter. Further to the west, the highest ground of that spot whereon *Stonehenge* stands eclipses a distant view, and *there* are the nearest barrows planted with rabbits, which do much damage too at *Stonehenge*, and threaten no less than the ruin of the whole. Upon the *vallum* of *Stonehenge* is one of the stones there, which seems to be a small altar, for some kind of <term>libations<note>Ritual pourings of wine or other liquids as an offering to a deity.</note></term>, and at the letter A. the mark of a cavity; of which more particularly, in the next page. The next or south-west prospect,
<margin>Table IX.</margin>
Table IX. from *Stonehenge*, takes in the country from *Berwickbarn*, and my Lord *Pembroke*’s wood of *Groveley*, to *Salisbury* steeple: a chain of barrows reaching a 6th part of the whole horizon. Many, from the great quantity of these sepulchral <term>tumuli<note>Another word for burial mounds or barrows.</note></term> here, injudiciously conclude that there have been great battles upon the plain, and that the slain were buried there. But they are really no other than family burying-places, set near this temple, for the same reason as we bury in church-yards and consecrated ground. *Salisbury* steeple seen from hence, brings to my sorrowful remembrance the great *Thomas* Earl of *Pembroke*, whose noble ashes are there deposited. He was patron of my studies, particularly those relating to *Stonehenge*. Virtue, piety, magnanimity, learning, generosity, all sublime qualities recommended and added to his illustrious descent. Glorious it will be for me if these pages live to testify to another age the intimacy he was pleased to honour me with.
