# STONEHENGE

<margin><term>Table X.<note>Refers to the tenth illustrated plate in the original volume.</note></term></margin>
In this *Plate*, the reader may notice another of the cavities within the <term>vallum<note>A rampart or earthwork wall made of soil and chalk.</note></term>, which corresponds to the one on the opposite side previously mentioned. The south-east prospect completes the circle, <term>Table X.</term> looking toward the valley to the south. This is where the rainwater flows from the entire site of *Stonehenge*, the vast stretch of the <term>Cursus<note>A large, ancient earthwork enclosure near Stonehenge, resembling a Roman racetrack.</note></term>, and the landscape beyond as far as the North Long Barrow; from there, it is carried into the River *Avon* at the village of *Lake*. The road between King Barrow and the Seven Barrows leads toward *Vespasian's* camp and on to *Amesbury*. The barrow located beneath those seven "kings" (which are of a later design) is the one closest to *Stonehenge*. Undoubtedly, water was used in the sacrifices and ceremonies performed here, and I have observed that most of our *Druid* temples are situated near rivers. The reason *Stonehenge* was not built near a river is likely what has effectively preserved it until now; this area was uninhabitable for lack of water and was situated too far from any town for easy farming. However, as I carefully studied the beauty and layout of this outer court, I noticed two remarkable spots that clearly align with the two stones set upon the *vallum*—stones that have puzzled all previous researchers. These details seem to explain one another, especially when compared to a coin found in the works of *Vaillant* <note>Jean Foy-Vaillant (1632–1706), a prominent French numismatist and historian.</note>, volume II, page 240. For this reason, I had it engraved on that plate, <term>Table XXIII.</term>, which shows the area of *Stonehenge*.

<margin>Table XXIII.</margin>
It is a coin of *Philip* the *Roman* emperor, minted by the city of *Heliopolis* <note>The "City of the Sun," modern-day Baalbek in Lebanon.</note> in *Coele-Syria* at the foot of Mount *Lebanon*, now called *Baalbek*. An impressive ancient temple still stands there, described and illustrated in *Maundrell’s* <note>Henry Maundrell (1663–1701), an English academic and traveler who wrote a famous account of his journey to Jerusalem.</note> travels to the Holy Land. In its walls are two or three stones of immense length, which appear to be fragments of an <term>obelisk<note>A tall, four-sided, tapering monument ending in a pyramid-like shape.</note></term> dedicated to the sun, from which *Heliopolis* takes its name. The coin depicts a temple built upon a rock, which is reached by steps. The temple is enclosed in an area by a wall. On the left side, following the curve of the *area*, is a stone altar. A little further along is a large vase for the water used in sacrifices. The inscription reads <term>Colonia Iul. Avg. Felix Heliopolitana<note>Original Latin: "The Julian, Augustan, Happy Colony of Heliopolis."</note></term>. Now, the two cavities in the circuit of our *area* at Stonehenge were very likely the places where two large stone vases were positioned, and the two stones were altars for specific rites, which we do not presume to fully explain. See another coin, number II, in *Descamps’ Selectiora Numismata* <note>Translated: "Selected Coins." A reference to a catalog of ancient coins.</note>, page 23, which suggests the same thing. Those stones are placed in their proper positions in my diagram of the *area of Stonehenge*, and I leave them to the better interpretations of those learned in these matters.

<margin>Table XXIII.</margin>
Mr. *Webb* <note>John Webb (1611–1672), an architect who argued that Stonehenge was a Roman temple of the Tuscan order.</note> imagines them to be the side-posts of two portals for two entrances, in addition to the main entrance. He uses them to support his imaginary triangles, upon which he designs the structure of *Stonehenge* based on a <term>Vitruvian plan<note>A design based on the principles of the Roman architect Vitruvius, emphasizing symmetry and geometry.</note></term>. To achieve this, he draws one stone—the one toward the east, or on the left hand of the true and only entrance—no less than 120 feet out of its actual position. No doubt the reader will be surprised by this, and will more easily believe me when I say his ground-plan in other parts is very far from being accurate. The reader will see from my diagram that the two semicircular hollows marked A A, where I suppose the water vases were placed, are positioned alternately with the two stones. I do not pretend to know why the *Druids* did this. However, that standing stone, together with the upper hollow marked A and the center of the grand entrance (by the stone that lies flat there), form a perfect <term>equilateral triangle<note>A triangle with all three sides of equal length.</note></term>. Yet, these features actually have no relation to the overall design of *Stonehenge* or the central <term>cell<note>The inner sanctum or central stone structure.</note></term>. Nor do these stones or hollows indicate any other entrance across the ditch into the area. Similarly, in the Tabernacle of *Moses* and the Temple of *Solomon*, large brass vases were set out for water in the court before the temple.

# DESCRIBED.
## CHAPTER III.

*The measurement of the ground plan and the outer circle of the temple, and the lintels <note>original: "imposts"; the horizontal stones spanning the tops of the uprights.</note> above it. Concerning the principal line of the work, running down the avenue and the single entrance into the area or court. The lintels are joined exquisitely by mortise and tenon. The temple at Persepolis <note>An ancient ceremonial capital of the Persian Empire, known for its monumental stone architecture.</note> is a building of this sort.*

LET us now set about an examination of the measures of the temple itself. Take a staff 10 feet 4 and 3/4 inches long. Divide it into six equal parts. These are the cubits of the ancients. Each cubit is divided into six parts. These are palms. Thus have we the original measure of the founders of *Stonehenge*. We will take Mr. *Webb*’s <note>John Webb (1611–1672) was an English architect who published "The Most Notable Antiquity of Great Britain, Vulgarly Called Stone-Heng," based on the notes of his teacher, Inigo Jones.</note> measures, and compare them herewith. <margin>PLATE XI.</margin> The ground plan. Mr. *Webb* says, on page 55, that the whole work of *Stonehenge*, being of a circular form, is 110 feet in diameter. But to be precise, it is 108 and somewhat more, and his own scale in his ground plan shows the same. This is the diameter from outside to outside, which in our ground plan is the principal diameter. The thickness of the stones of the outward circle, he says on page 59, are 3 feet and a half. Hence the inner diameter becomes almost 102 feet *English*. If the reader pleases to measure 102 feet upon the comparative scales which I gave of the *English* foot and *Hebrew* cubit—being the measure used by the *Druids* <note>The author attributes the construction to the Druids, the high-ranking priestly class in ancient Celtic cultures, though modern archaeology dates the site much earlier.</note>—or in the scales at the bottom of the ground plan, he will find that it amounts exactly to 60 cubits; 30 cubits being the radius wherewith they struck the circle upon the turf, which is the inner circumference of that work. That sufficiently defined their ground plan. For though they intended in general that the thickness of the stones of this outer circle should be 3 feet and a half—but to speak more properly, 2 cubits (which is the same measure)—yet they were more careful of one side only of that dimension. And as the chief business was within the temple, they set the best face of the stones inwards, upon that ground-line; the other face was suited as well as the stone fragments <note>original: "scantlings"</note> they could get best answered. <margin>PLATE VI.</margin> *Webb*’s 3 feet and a half is precisely 3 feet 5 inches and somewhat more, making completely 2 *Druid* cubits, as you find by the scales. They that carefully view *Stonehenge* will easily see that the stones of the inside, both of the outward circle and of the cell, are the smoothest, best wrought, and have the handsomest appearance. For so the polite architects of the eastern part of the world bestowed more elegance within their temples than without. They were not like our modern *London* builders, who carve every molding and crowd every ornament—which they borrow out of books—onto the outside of our public structures, so that they may more conveniently gather the dust and smoke. The truth is, good sense and the observation of nature produce the same ideas in all ages and all nations. Our *Druids* observed that God Almighty, in forming the body of a man, made all the external parts great, bold, and round, with sufficient ornament; but where the beauty chiefly consisted was in the fitness of the proportions, in symmetry and plainness. In the inside, he has displayed all the minute details <note>original: "minutiæ"</note> of divine skill. They have done the like, according to their way, in *Stonehenge*. So even as to the outward appearance, I find they took care to set those stones that had the best outward face toward the front or entrance. And to complicate the general scheme of the work, they made use of two centers instead of one, but at a distance of 2 cubits from one another; perhaps to make the thing intricate and as if magical, besides the advantage it gives to the oval form of the included cell.

### DESCRIBED.

F

of surcharging them. We see here plain, natural, easy geometry—what we may call the first rudiment of art—deduced from common reason. However, to have achieved a <note>The author uses "Roman delicacy" to mean the refined, precise aesthetic of Classical architecture.</note> **Roman delicacy** in this matter, they must first, and by the nature of the thing, have possessed a much finer eye and taste than I can claim to have. 

The Druids had, since patriarchal times, constructed their altars or temples from rude, unpolished stones. But upon hearing, probably from *Phoenician* traders, of the glories of *Solomon’s* temple—or at least of other temples made artfully in imitation of it, such as those of *Sesostris* in *Egypt* and others around *Phoenicia*—they made a small attempt at squared measurements and wrought stones. This seems to have been their first and last work of this kind that I have heard of, whether in the *British* isles or on the European continent. 

No doubt this work gave them such a high reputation that even the people of *Gaul* <note>Modern-day France and surrounding regions.</note> themselves could not help admitting to *Caesar* that the training of these men first began here in Britain. It was carried out with such success that they sent their youth from the continent here, as if to an academy, to be initiated into their learning. We should not take these words too strictly, as if the Druids here began an entirely new Oriental style of religion; rather, what the Gauls had of it was derived from *Britain*. It appeared as new to them—who were chiefly idolaters—just as in many preceding ages, *Abraham’s* religion appeared new to the inhabitants of *Phoenicia*, being so different from those beliefs tainted by idolatry. 

Probably, the Druids did not have much opportunity to build another work like *Stonehenge* between its foundation and the *Roman* times. I suspect that the encroachments of the *Gallic* nations from the continent, settling themselves in *Britain* about 200 years before *Caesar’s* invasion, greatly molested the Druids in these southern counties and drove them, along with the ancient *Britons*, further northward and westward. But we will treat this more particularly later, when we offer our opinion on the time when it was built.

<margin>Plate 12.</margin>
In the orthographic plate <note>A technical architectural drawing showing the elevations and proportions of the structure without the distortion of perspective.</note>, *Plate 12*, we can see the strict geometry of the work of this outer circle. We also see the artful variation from that geometry, made in order to make the opening of the grand entrance somewhat wider than the rest. *Mr. Webb* <note>John Webb (1611–1672), an architect who published a book arguing that Stonehenge was a Roman temple built to the Tuscan order.</note> does not notice this detail; he might have celebrated it, for it is nothing less than a <term>Vitruvian rule</term> <note>Named after Vitruvius, a Roman architect whose writings defined the "correct" proportions for classical buildings.</note> to relax the <term>intercolumniation</term> <note>The distance between two columns or pillars.</note> exactly in the middle of the portico, in the front of a temple and directly across from the door. 

He speaks of it in *Book 3, chapter 2*, when discussing the <term>Eustyle ratio</term> <note>original: "Eustyle ratio"; a Greek architectural term for the "ideal" spacing between columns, typically 2 and 1/4 column diameters apart.</note>, which is considered the best for use, appearance, and strength. He directs the spacing between columns to be two and a quarter diameters, but the middle spacing to be three diameters. By this means, the approach to the door will be much more convenient, without losing any beauty in its appearance. And this is exactly the reality of the case before us.

But alas, our *British* priests knew nothing of *Vitruvius*. They deduced this "knack" from an authority much older than him: namely, from pure natural reason and good sense. Nor does this adjustment hurt the rest of the work. The opening strictly ought to have been two cubits <note>An ancient unit of measurement based on the length of the forearm.</note> wide like the rest, but they increased it to two and a half cubits. This only crowds the next intervals on each side slightly closer together, while the rest preserve their true distance all the way around. 

In the work itself, this is obvious enough to the naked eye. Furthermore, there is another remarkable detail observed by our priests. Because the opening of the principal entrance we are speaking of is wider than the rest, they have made the <term>impost</term> <note>The horizontal lintel stone that sits on top of the vertical pillars.</note> over it thicker than the rest, which is also obvious to the naked eye. This was done to effectively secure it from breaking under its own weight. However, they placed this additional thickness on the bottom side. They were aware that putting it on top would have produced an ugly effect by breaking the level line of that noble <term>cincture</term> <note>The continuous ring of stones forming the top of the circle.</note>. It must be admitted that this was extremely well adjusted. The breadth of the stone that hangs overhead in this place is astonishing. See *Plate 7*, called "a peep into <margin>Plate 7.</margin>
the

He suggests these innovations were influenced by Phoenician reports of Solomon's Temple and predated the Roman occupation of Britain.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's personal account of his 1723 survey of Stonehenge with Lord Winchelsea, transitioning from the previous page's discussion on the origins of Druidic geometry to a detailed physical description of the site's measurements and structural integrity.</meta>

->S T O N E H E N G E<-

...the **Holy of Holies** <note>original: "sanctum sanctorum," the innermost and most sacred part of a temple.</note>. I had the greatest pleasure imaginable, in July of the year 1723, in being here for several days together with the learned Heneage, Lord *Winchelsea*. I have good reason to boast of the intimacy he allowed me; and his memory must forever be dear to me for his noble qualities.

<margin>Plate VII.</margin> My Lord and I were very careful in taking the measurements of *Stonehenge*; and with great grief we observed the stones represented in <margin>Plate V.</margin> that *Plate*, and *Plate* V. (the front view), to be leaning significantly forward from their true vertical position, and in the utmost danger of falling. It is to be feared that some reckless people have been digging about the great entrance with ridiculous hopes of finding treasure, thereby loosening the chalky foundation. We found by measurement that the upper edge of the **lintel** <term>impost: a horizontal stone resting on vertical pillars.</term> overhangs by no less than 2 feet 7 inches, which is a significant amount for a height of 18 feet. The whole width at the foundation is only 3 and a half feet. This noble front is now mainly kept upright by the masonry of the **mortise** <term>mortaise: a hole or socket cut into a stone to receive a projection from another stone.</term> and **tenon** <term>tenon: a projecting piece of stone made to fit into a mortise hole, similar to a LEGO peg.</term> of the lintels.

Through the middle of the principal entrance runs the main line of the entire work: the diameter from north-east to south-west. This line cuts through the middle of the altar, the length of the central chamber, the entrance, the entrance into the court, and so runs down the middle of the **approach road** <note>The "avenue" refers to the long, banked path leading to the monument.</note> to the bottom of the valley for almost 2,000 feet. This is very apparent to anyone at first sight and confirms this as the only primary entrance of the temple. None of the other gaps between the stones of the outer circle hold any special importance. There is no such thing as "three entrances," as *Mr. Webb’s* <note>John Webb (1611–1672) was an architect who argued that Stonehenge was a Roman temple dedicated to Coelus.</note> plan suggests. He might as well have claimed there are six, as that is how many points of his triangles meet the gaps at the edge of the outer circle. All the principal centers that make up the work lie upon this line; it deviates only slightly from true north-east.

The skill of our ancient builders in creating mortises and tenons between the upright stones and the lintels is admirable, but so unlike any practice of the *Romans* that this alone is enough to disprove their claim to the work. Much judgment and good sense is shown in their management. The centers of the tenons are 2 **cubits** <note>An ancient unit of length based on the forearm; the author believes the Druids used a specific "Druid cubit."</note> apart on each upright. By this means, there is a distance of 4 cubits from the center of the tenon of one stone to the center of the tenon of its neighbor, across the gaps or within a single lintel. If one divides the top face of an upright into two squares, the center of a tenon is in the center of that square. If one divides the underside of a lintel into three squares, the corresponding mortises are in the centers of the two outermost squares. This was the strict geometrical method used by the founders, so the stones fitted as soon as they were placed in their true positions. These tenons and mortises of the outer circle are round and fit one another very well. The tenons and mortises are 10 and a half inches in diameter, which is 3 **palms** <note>A unit of length based on the width of a hand.</note> or half a cubit. They resemble half an egg rather than a perfect hemisphere. These most effectively keep both uprights and lintels from shifting out of place, and it must have required great effort for those who threw them down. *Sir Robert Sibbald* <note>A Scottish antiquarian and physician.</note> mentions a **rocking stone** in *Ireland* designed with a mortise and tenon like ours; *Mr. Toland* <note>John Toland (1670–1722), an Irish rationalist philosopher who wrote extensively on the Druids.</note> provides an account of this and others like it, identifying them as the works of the *Druids*.

<margin>Plate XII.</margin> The total height of the upright and lintel combined is 10 and a half cubits. The uprights are 9 cubits and the lintel is 1 and a half cubits, so the lintel is one-sixth the height of the upright. If we measure the outside, the combined width of two upright stones and the gap between them is 10 and a half cubits—equal to the total height—and the gap is half the width of a stone, while the thickness of a stone is half its width. As we mentioned, the lintel lying over the grand entrance was deeper and longer than the others. *Abraham Sturges*, an architect, and I measured it in the presence of Lord *Winchelsea*. Its middle length is 11 feet 10 inches (6 cubits and 4 palms) and its height is 2 feet 11 inches (1 cubit and 4 palms).

<summary>This page details the author's survey of Stonehenge, describing the structural engineering of the mortise and tenon joints and arguing that these features—unique from Roman methods—prove the monument was built by Druids. Stukeley also expresses concern over the monument's stability due to treasure hunters damaging the foundations.</summary>
<keywords>Stonehenge survey, Lord Winchelsea, mortise and tenon, Druid architecture, ancient measurements, cubit, palm, monument preservation, John Webb</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the detailed architectural description of Stonehenge's outer circle and its comparison to other ancient structures.</meta>

### DESCRIBED.

The builders added slightly to the width of these stones—more so than the others—measuring 3 feet 9 inches, which equals two <term>cubits</term><note>An ancient unit of measurement based on the length of the forearm, approximately 18 to 22 inches.</note> and a <term>palm</term><note>An ancient unit of measurement based on the width of a hand, roughly 3 inches.</note>. **Note:** The scale of my drawing is designed for the inside of the circle, which serves as the basis for the geometric proportions. Consequently, the outer width of the vertical stones and the lengths of the <term>imposts</term><note>The horizontal lintel stones that rest across the tops of two vertical stones.</note> are somewhat larger than they appear on the scale. Any knowledgeable reader will understand that this is the natural result of measuring arcs within a larger circle.

Nothing in nature could be based on a simpler concept than this vast circle of stones and its <term>corona</term><note>Literally a "crown"; referring here to the ring of horizontal stones at the top.</note> or crown-work. Yet, the effect is truly majestic and inspires a sense of ancient awe, which is the primary requirement for sacred buildings. While a single stone is a marvel in itself, the bold presence and the striking relief of the entire <term>compages</term><note>The structure or complex as a whole.</note> can only be fully understood by seeing the original. On the outside, the top stones are slightly rounded to follow the curve of the circle, but on the inside, they are straight—though ideally, they should have been slightly curved. This makes them somewhat thicker in the middle than at the ends, and wider than the two cubits that mark the thickness of the upright stones on an <term>ichnography</term><note>An architectural ground plan or map showing the horizontal layout of the structure.</note>. Thus, from the inside, the crown-work forms a polygon of 30 sides. This subtle technique does not diminish the beauty of the work in the least; rather, it adds significant strength to the whole structure and to the horizontal stones in particular. We can surmise their proportions were well-chosen, as so many of them have been violently toppled yet did not break upon falling. Their greater width in the center, where they bridge the gaps between pillars, adds to the solemnity of the site by casting deep shadows below. The method of jointing used in this building is very curious and appears to be the oldest and only surviving example of such work in the world. As far as I know, nothing else compares <margin>Plate XXXV.</margin> to it except the famous ruins of **Persepolis**. (See Plate XXXV.) That site is composed of great stones laid across one another like **Stonehenge**, but it does not use the <term>mortise and tenon</term><note>A joint where a projecting "tenon" on one stone fits into a hollow "mortise" hole in another, much like Lego blocks or woodworking joints.</note> method. Both common people and scholars generally assume those ruins are the remains of the palace of the **Persian** kings, burned by **Alexander the Great**; however, it is actually an open-air temple like ours, built in much the same way. The stones there are well-squared and decorated with carvings and moldings, and the structures are square rather than circular. **Persepolis** represents a transition between the ancient, patriarchal round form of open-air temples and the square form introduced under the **Jewish dispensation**<note>The era of religious history defined by the laws given to the Israelites, which favored square layouts for the Tabernacle and Temple to distinguish them from pagan circular sites.</note>, which was established to oppose the older circular style that had often fallen into idolatry. But I shall perhaps speak more of this later when I discuss the most ancient temples.

Of the outer circle at **Stonehenge**, which originally consisted of 60 stones (30 uprights and 30 horizontal stones), more than half of the uprights—specifically 17—are still standing. Eleven of these remain in a continuous row by the grand entrance, with five horizontal stones still resting upon them. One upright at the back of the temple (on the southwest side) leans against a stone from the inner circle. Six more stones lie on the ground, either whole or in pieces. Thus, 24 out of the 30 original uprights are still visible on site. However, only one other horizontal stone remains in its proper place, and only two others lie on the ground, meaning 22 have been carried away. From this, I infer that the temple was not destroyed when Christianity first took hold, but rather that some crude and sacrilegious hands carted the stones away for other construction needs. Nonetheless, it brings the highest possible pleasure to a disciplined mind to walk around and contemplate these stately ruins, which I <margin>Plate XIII.</margin> have attempted to preserve in the exterior drawings, such as Plate XIII (viewed from the southwest), and others. But as the Roman poet **Lucan** wrote:

> ->*Now more and more, headlong fate drives all things to ruin.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Jam magis atque magis præceps agit omnia fatum."</note>

The author compares the site's construction to the ruins of Persepolis and laments the historical theft of the stones for common use.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the detailed architectural survey of Stonehenge, specifically focusing on the measurements and structural logic of the outer circle of stones.</meta>

# DESCRIBED.

<term>palms<note>An ancient unit of length based on the width of a human hand, roughly 3 inches or 7.5 cm.</note></term>. They have likewise added a little to its breadth, more than the rest, being 3 foot 9 inches, which is 2 <term>cubits<note>A unit of measurement based on the length of the forearm, approximately 18 to 21 inches.</note></term> and a palm. **N. B.** The scale of my drawing is adapted for the inside of the circle, upon which the proportions in geometry are built: so that the outward breadths of the uprights and lengths of the <term>imposts<note>The horizontal stones (lintels) that lie across the top of the vertical uprights.</note></term> are somewhat more, than by the scale appears there. The intelligent reader knows this must be the consequence, in arcs of a larger circle.

<margin>PLATE XXXV.</margin>
Nothing in nature could be of a more simple idea than this vast circle of stones, and its crown-work or *<term>corona<note>Latin for "crown"; referring to the top ring of stones.</note></term>* at top; and yet its effect is truly majestic and venerable, which is the main requisite in sacred structures. A single stone is a thing worthy of admiration, but the boldness and great relievo of the whole *<term>compages<note>A Latin term for a complex structure or the joining together of parts.</note></term>*, can only be rightly apprehended from a view of the original. On the outside, the imposts are rounded a little to accommodate the curve of the circle, and within they are straight, though they ought to be a little curved. This makes them somewhat broader in the middle than at the end, and broader than the 2 cubits, which is the thickness of the upright stones, upon an <term>ichnography<note>A technical term for a ground plan or architectural map.</note></term>. So that within, the crown-work makes a polygon of 30 sides. But this little artifice, without debasing the beauty of the work in the least, adds much strength to the whole, and to the imposts in particular. We may guess their proportions are well chosen, when so many of them are thrown down by violence, and not broken in the fall. And their greater breadth in the middle, or that part that covers the intervals, adds to the solemnity of the place, by the shadow they present at the bottom. The whole affair of jointing in this building is very curious, and seems to be the oldest and only specimen of this kind of work in the world. There is nothing, that I know of, comes in competition with it, but the celebrated ruins of *Persepolis* <note>The ceremonial capital of the ancient Achaemenid Empire in modern-day Iran.</note>. Plate XXXV. It is composed of great stones laid across one another, as *Stonehenge*: but not with mortise and tenon <note>A joint where a projection (tenon) fits into a hole (mortise).</note>. The common people and the learned too, generally take it for the remains of the palace of the *Persian* monarchs, burnt by *Alexander* the Great; but it is really an open temple like ours, and made much in the same manner. But the stones are well squared, ornamented with mouldings and carvings, and the whole of them are squares, not round works as here. *Persepolis* is a mixture between the ancient patriarchal round form of open temples, and the square form introduced under the *Jewish* dispensation <note>The religious system or era established by Jewish law.</note>, in opposition to the former, which were generally degenerated into idolatrous purposes. But of this I shall speak more perhaps hereafter, when I treat of the most ancient temples.

<margin>PLATE XIII.</margin>
Of the outer circle at *Stonehenge* which in its perfection consisted of 60 stones (30 uprights and 30 imposts), there are more than half the uprights, namely 17, left standing. Eleven of these uprights remain continuous by the grand entrance, with five imposts upon them. One upright at the back of the temple or on the south-west, leans upon a stone of the inner circle. There are six more lying upon the ground, whole or in pieces. So that 24 out of 30 are still visible at the place. There is but one impost more in its proper place. And but two lying upon the ground, so that 22 are carried off. Hence I infer, this temple was not defaced when Christianity prevailed. But some rude and sacrilegious hands carried the stones away for other uses. However, it cannot but be the highest pleasure imaginable to a regular mind to walk round and contemplate the stately ruins which I have endeavored to preserve in the outside views, such as Plate XIII from the south-west, and so of the rest. But we may say with *Lucan* <note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus, a Roman poet.</note>,

->Now more and more, headlong fate drives all things. <note>original: "Jam magis atque magis præceps agit omnia fatum." — from Lucan's Pharsalia.</note><-

# STONEHENGE
## CHAP. IV.

->Regarding the smaller circle of stones, which lacks capping stones. A dispute against *Mr. Webb*.<-

<margin>PLATE XII.</margin>
Many drawings have been made and published of *Stonehenge*. However, they are not done in a scientific manner that might prove a specific point or improve our understanding of the work. I have therefore drawn four <term>elevation drawings<note>original: "architectonic orthographies"; these are technical drawings showing the vertical front and sides of a building to scale.</note></term>: one, <term>PLATE XII.</term>, shows the front and outside; three others show different sections along the two principal diameters of the work. These will forever preserve the memory of the site, even when the ruins of these ruins have perished; because from these and the ground plan, an exact model may be made at any time.

<margin>PLATES XIV, XV, XVI.</margin>
<margin>PLATE XI.</margin>
<term>PLATES XIV, XV, XVI.</term> these elevation drawings show the primary intent of the founders; they are the designs which the <term>Druids<note>An ancient priestly class in Britain and Gaul whom the author credits with building Stonehenge.</note></term> made before they executed the work. By comparing them with the corresponding drawings of the ruins, we gain a true idea of the place as it was in its perfection. But now, as we are about to enter the building, it will be proper to survey the ground plan again, <term>PLATE XI.</term>, which is so different from the one published by *Mr. Webb*. Instead of an imaginary hexagon, we see a most noble and beautiful <term>ellipse<note>original: "ellipsis"; an oval shape.</note></term>, which composes the cell—or, as he names it, I think <term>inner sanctuary<note>original: "adyttum"; the most sacred, private room in an ancient temple.</note></term> is a proper word. There is nothing like it, to my knowledge, in all antiquity; it is an original invention of our <term>Druids</term>, an ingenious contrivance to expand the inner and more sacred part where they performed their religious offices. The two outward circles do not hinder the sight, but add much to the solemnity of the place and the rituals by the fame and variety of their intervals. Those who were within, when it was in its perfection, would see a most notable effect produced by this elliptical figure, enclosed within a circular <term>crown-ring<note>original: "corona"; the outer circle of stones forming a "crown".</note></term>, having the large hemisphere of the heavens as its covering.

Somewhat more than 8 feet inward from the inside of this exterior circle is another circle of much smaller stones. In the measurements of the <term>Druids</term>, it is five <term>cubits<note>An ancient unit of length, roughly 18 to 20 inches, based on the length of a forearm.</note></term>. This circle was made with a radius of 24 <term>cubits</term>, drawn from the common centers of the work. This marked the line of the circumference in the chalk, where they set these stones. The stones that compose it are 40 in number, forming with the outward circle a kind of circular <term>covered walkway<note>original: "portico".</note></term>: a most beautiful walk with a lovely effect. 

<margin>PLATE XVII.</margin>
Something of its beauty may be seen in <term>Plate XVII.</term> where it is currently most perfect. We are deceived by *Mr. Webb’s* scheme, where he places only 30 stones (equal to the number of the outer circle) the better to indulge his fantasy of the <term>double-rowed appearance<note>original: "dipteric aspect".</note></term>, page 76. He attempts to persuade us that this is a <term>Roman work</term> composed of a mixture of the plainness and solidity of the <term>Tuscan style<note>original: "Tuscan order"; the simplest of the classical architectural styles.</note></term> with the delicacy of the <term>Corinthian style<note>original: "Corinthian"; the most ornate of the classical Greek/Roman architectural styles.</note></term>. He claims that in appearance it is <term>double-winged and open to the sky<note>original: "dipteros hypaethros"</note></term>, and in manner it is <term>closely-columned<note>original: "pycnostylos"</note></term>; which, when applied to our ancient monument, is no better than playing with words. For even if we suppose this inner circle consisted of only 30 stones, set as they are in his scheme on the same radius as those of the outer circle: what likeness does this have to a proper <term>covered walkway</term>, or to any architectural style—<term>Tuscan</term>, <term>Corinthian</term>, or any other? What similarity is there between these stones and a <term>column</term>? One sort is square and oblong, while the opposite ones (by his own account) are shaped like pyramids. Of what style is a <term>column</term>, or rather a <term>rectangular pillar<note>original: "pilaster".</note></term>, where its height is little more than twice its diameter? Where is the base, the shaft, the capital, or anything else that belongs to a <term>pillar</term>, <term>rectangular pillar</term>, or <term>walkway</term>? The truth and fact is this: the inner circle has 40 stones in it. Consequently, few or none of them correspond precisely with the two intervals on the principal diameter, except by chance.

<summary>This page details the measurements and layout of Stonehenge's inner circle, which the author argues consists of forty stones arranged in an ellipse rather than a hexagon. He vigorously refutes John Webb’s claims that the structure follows Roman architectural orders, asserting instead that it is a unique Druidic invention.</summary>

# STONEHENGE
## CHAPTER IV.
### *Regarding the smaller circle of stones, without lintels. A dispute against Mr. Webb.*

Many drawings of <term>Stonehenge</term> <note>The famous prehistoric monument on Salisbury Plain, which the author attributes to the Druids.</note> have been made and published. However, they are not done in a scientific manner that might prove a point or improve our understanding of the construction. I have, therefore, drawn four <term>architectural elevations</term> <note>Original: "architectonic orthographies"; these are drawings showing the vertical front or side view of a building to scale.</note>: <margin>PLATE XII.</margin> one, Plate XII, shows the front and exterior; three others show different sections along the two primary diameters of the work. These will forever preserve the memory of the site, even when the ruins of these ruins have perished; because from these and the <term>ground plan</term> <note>A map showing the layout of the stones as they sit on the earth.</note>, an exact model may be made at any time. <margin>PLATES XIV, XV, XVI.</margin> Plates XIV, XV, and XVI—these <term>elevations</term> show the primary intent of the founders; they are the designs which the <term>Druids</term> <note>The priestly and learned class of the ancient Celts, whom the author believes built Stonehenge.</note> made before they executed the work. By comparing them with the corresponding drawings of the ruins, we gain a true idea of the place as it was in its perfection. But now, <margin>PLATE XI.</margin> as we are about to enter the building, it is proper to again survey the <term>ground plan</term>, Plate XI, which is so different from the one published by Mr. Webb <note>John Webb (1611–1672), an architect who published a book claiming Stonehenge was a Roman temple.</note>. Instead of an imaginary <term>hexagon</term> <note>A six-sided shape.</note>, we see a most noble and beautiful <term>ellipse</term> <note>An oval shape.</note>, which makes up the "cell," as he calls it; I think <term>adytum</term> <note>A Greek term for the innermost, most sacred sanctuary of a temple, where only priests were allowed.</note> is a more proper word. There is nothing like it, to my knowledge, in all of antiquity; it is an original invention of our Druids, an ingenious contrivance to open up the inner and more sacred part where they performed their religious offices. The two outward circles do not block the view, but add much to the solemnity of the place and its rituals by the <note>Original: "crebrity," meaning the frequency or density of the stones.</note> density and variety of their spacing. Those who stood within, when it was in its perfection, would have seen a most remarkable effect produced by this elliptical figure, enclosed within a circular <term>corona</term> <note>Literally a "crown"; the outer ring of stones.</note>, having the large hemisphere of the heavens as its roof.

A little more than 8 feet inward from the inside of this exterior circle is another circle of much smaller stones. In the measurement used by the Druids, it is five <term>cubits</term> <note>An ancient unit of length based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger.</note>. This circle was made with a radius of 24 cubits, drawn from the common centers of the work. This marked the line in the chalk circumference where they set these stones. The stones that compose it are 40 in number, forming with the outward circle a sort of circular <term>portico</term> <note>A porch or walkway consisting of a roof supported by columns.</note>: a most beautiful walk with a lovely effect. <margin>PLATE XVII.</margin> Something of its beauty may be seen in <term>Plate XVII</term>, where it is currently most intact. We are being misled by Mr. Webb’s diagram, where he places only 30 stones (equal to the number of the outer circle) just to satisfy his whim regarding the <term>dipteric layout</term> <note>Original: "dipteric aspect," referring to a classical temple surrounded by a double row of columns.</note>, p. 76. He tries to persuade us that this is a <term>Roman work</term> composed of a mixture of the plainness and solidity of the <term>Tuscan order</term> <note>The simplest of the five classical orders of architecture, known for its unadorned columns.</note> with the delicacy of the <term>Corinthian</term> <note>The most ornate of the classical orders, characterized by slender columns and capitals decorated with acanthus leaves.</note>. He claims that in appearance it is <term>dipteros hypaethros</term> <note>Greek: a building with a double row of columns that is open to the sky.</note> and in style it is <term>pycnostyle</term> <note>Greek: an architectural arrangement where columns are placed very close together.</note>; which, when applied to our ancient monument, is nothing more than playing with words. For suppose this inner circle consisted of only 30 stones, set as they are in his diagram on the same radius as those of the outer circle: what similarity does this have to a proper <term>portico</term>, or to any architectural <term>order</term> like the Tuscan or Corinthian? What likeness is there between these stones and a <term>column</term>? In his own account, one type is a square oblong, and the opposite one is pyramidal. In what <term>order</term> is a <term>column</term>, or rather a <term>pilaster</term> <note>A rectangular column, especially one projecting from a wall.</note>, found where its height is little more than twice its diameter? Where is the base, the shaft, the capital, or anything else that belongs to a <term>pillar</term>, <term>pilaster</term>, or <term>portico</term>? The truth and the facts are these: the inner circle has 40 stones in it. Consequently, few or none of them (except for the two openings on the main diameter) happen to align precisely with—

### STONEHENGE
## CHAPTER IV.
->Concerning the lesser circle of stones, without horizontal lintels. An argument against Mr. Webb.<-

<margin>Plate XII</margin>
Many drawings have been made and published of *Stonehenge*. However, they are not done in a scientific manner that can prove any specific point or improve our understanding of the work. I have therefore drawn four <term>architectural elevations <note>original: "architectonic orthographies"; these are scale drawings showing the vertical front or side of a building</note></term>: one, <term>Plate XII</term>, shows the front and the exterior; three others show different cross-sections along the two main diameters of the structure. These will forever preserve the memory of the site, even when the ruins of these ruins have finally perished; because, using these and the ground plan, an exact model could be made at any time.

<margin>Plates XIV, XV, XVI</margin>
<term>Plates XIV, XV, and XVI</term>: these elevations show the primary intent of the founders; they are the designs which the Druids <note>The author, William Stukeley, was a pioneer in archaeology who famously (though incorrectly by modern standards) attributed Stonehenge to the ancient Celtic Druids</note> made before they put the work into execution. By comparing them with the corresponding drawings of the current ruins, we gain a true idea of how the place looked when it was in its state of perfection.

<margin>Plate XI</margin>
But now, as we are about to enter the building, it is proper to again survey the ground plan, <term>Plate XI</term>, which is so different from the one published by Mr. Webb. Instead of an imaginary hexagon <note>John Webb argued the stones were arranged in a six-sided Roman pattern</note>, we see a most noble and beautiful ellipsis <note>an oval shape</note>, which forms the "cell," as he calls it; though I think <term>inner sanctuary <note>original Latin: "adytum"</note></term> is a more proper word. There is nothing like it, to my knowledge, in all of antiquity; it is an original invention of our Druids, an ingenious contrivance to open up the inner and more sacred part where they performed their religious offices. The two outward circles do not block the sight, but add much to the solemnity of the place and the rituals through the shortness and variety of the spaces between the stones. Those who stood within, when it was in its perfection, would see a most remarkable effect produced by this elliptical figure, enclosed within a circular <term>crown <note>original Latin: "corona"</note></term>, with the great hemisphere of the heavens serving as its roof.

A little more than 8 feet inward from the inside of this exterior circle is another circle of much smaller stones. In the measurement used by the Druids, this is five <term>cubits <note>an ancient unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note></term>. This circle was created using a radius of 24 cubits, drawn from the shared centers of the work. This marked the line in the chalk circumference where they placed these stones. The stones that compose it are 40 in number, forming—along with the outward circle—a sort of circular <term>covered walkway <note>original: "portico"</note></term>: a most beautiful path with a lovely effect. Something of its beauty may be seen in <term>Plate XVII</term>,

<margin>Plate XVII</margin>
where it is currently most well-preserved. We are deceived by Mr. Webb’s diagram, where he places only 30 stones (matching the number of the outer circle) merely to satisfy his whim regarding the <term>double-winged appearance <note>original: "dipteric aspect"</note></term> (page 76). He tries to persuade us that this is a Roman work composed of a mixture of the plainness and solidity of the <term>Tuscan order</term> combined with the delicacy of the <term>Corinthian</term>. He claims that in appearance it is <term>double-colonnaded and open to the sky <note>original Latin/Greek: "dipteros hypæthros"</note></term> and in style it is <term>tightly-columned <note>original Greek: "pycnostylos"</note></term>; when applied to our ancient monument, this is nothing more than playing with words. For even if we suppose this inner circle consisted of only 30 stones, set as they are in his diagram on the same radius as the outer ones, what likeness does this have to a proper walkway, or to the Tuscan or Corinthian orders? What similarity is there between these stones and a column? One type is a square oblong, while the other—by his own account—is pyramidal. Of what "order" is a column, or rather a <term>pillar <note>original: "pilaster"</note></term>, where the height is little more than twice its diameter? Where is the base, the shaft, the capital, or anything else that belongs to a pillar or a porch? The truth and the fact is this: the inner circle has 40 stones in it. Consequently, few or none of the stones, except for those two gaps on the main diameter, happen to align precisely with—

<summary>The author argues that Stonehenge was designed by Druids as an elliptical sanctuary rather than a Roman hexagonal temple. He critiques John Webb's architectural theories, pointing out that the physical measurements and the number of stones (40 vs. 30) do not support Roman classical "orders."</summary>

<meta>This page continues a detailed architectural analysis of Stonehenge, specifically focusing on the inner circle of stones and contrasting the author's observations with the earlier theories of John Webb.</meta>

### DESCRIBED.

with those of the outer circle. Whereby a much better effect is produced, than if the case had been as *Webb* <note>John Webb (1611–1672) was an architect and son-in-law to Inigo Jones; he published a work arguing that Stonehenge was a Roman temple.</note> would have it. For a regularity there, would have been trifling and impertinent. Again, Mr. *Webb* makes these stones pyramidal in shape, without reason. They are truly flat parallelograms, as those of the outer circle. He says, p. 59, they are one foot and a half in breadth, but they are twice as much. Their general and designed proportion is 2 cubits, or two cubits and a half, as they happened to find suitable stones. <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of measure, approximately 18 to 20 inches, based on the length of the forearm.</note> A radius of 23 cubits strikes the inner circumference: of 24 the outer. They are, as we said before, a cubit thick, and 4 cubits and a half in height, which is above 7 foot. This was their stated proportion, being every way the half of the outer uprights. Such seems to have been the original purpose of the founders, though it is not very precise, neither in design, nor execution. In some places, the stones are broader than the intervals, in some otherwise: so that in the ground-plot I chose to mark them as equal, each 2 cubits and a half. There are scarce any of these entire, as to all these dimensions; but from all, and from the symmetry of these *Celtic* kind of works, which I have been conversant in, I found this to be the intention of the authors. It is easy for any one to satisfy themselves, they never were *pyramidal*; for behind the upper end of the <term>inner sanctuary</term> <note>original: "adytum"; the most sacred innermost part of a temple.</note>, there are three or four left, much broader than thick, above twice; and not the least semblance of a pyramid. I doubt not but he means an obelisk, to which they might some of them possibly be likened, but not at all to a pyramid. Nor indeed do I imagine any thing of an obelisk was in the founder’s view; but the stones diminish a little upward, as common reason dictates they ought to do. Nor need we bestow the pompous words of either pyramid, or obelisk upon them. For they cannot be said to imitate, either one or other, in shape, use, much less magnitude: the chief thing to be regarded, in a comparison of this sort. The central distance between these stones of the inner circle, measured upon their outward circumference, is 4 cubits. I observe further, that the two stones of the principal entrance of this circle, correspondent to that of the outer circle, are broader and taller, and set at a greater distance from each other, being rather more than that of the principal entrance in the outer circle. It is evident too, that they are set somewhat more inward than the rest; so as that their outward face stands on the line that marks the inner circumference of the inner circle. I know no reason for all this, unless it be, that the outside of these two stones, is the outside of the hither end of the ellipsis of the <term>inner sanctuary</term> <note>original: "adytum"</note>: for so it corresponds by measure upon the ground-plot. This is apparent, that they eminently point out the principal entrance of that circle, which is also the entrance into the <term>inner sanctuary</term>. For five stones on this hand, and five on that, are as it were the <term>screens</term> <note>original: "cancelli"; lattice-work or railings that separate the choir or sanctuary from the main body of a church.</note> between the <term>holy place</term> <note>original: "sanctum"</note> and <term>holy of holies</term> <note>original: "sanctum sanctorum"</note>, if we may use such expressions. It is scarce worth mentioning to the reader, that there never were any imposts <note>Horizontal lintel stones placed across the tops of upright stones.</note> over the heads of these stones of the inner circle. They are sufficiently fastened into the ground. Such would have been no security to them, no ornament. They are of a harder kind of stone than the rest, as they are lesser; the better to resist violence.

There are but nineteen of the whole number left; but eleven of them are standing <term>in their original positions</term> <note>original: "in situ"</note>. There are five in one place standing contiguous, three in another, two in another. The walk between these two circles, which is 300 foot in circumference, is very noble and very delightful. Probably it gave *Inigo Jones* <note>A famous English architect (1573–1652) who was one of the first to conduct a survey of Stonehenge.</note> the idea of designing that fine circular portico, which is one great beauty, among many, in his drawings for *Whitehall* <note>The Palace of Whitehall in London, which Jones designed for King James I.</note>, published lately from the originals by my Lord *Burlington*; who has a true notion of the extraordinary merit of that great man: and very commendably has revived his memory. Such a circular portico put in execution, would have a marvellous effect, much exceed a common gallery in use, because it is a perpetual walk, without turning back, and well becomes a royal residence.

G

<meta>This page continues the architectural analysis of Stonehenge, specifically criticizing John Webb’s theory that the monument followed classical Roman or Greek designs. The author argues that the layout of the stones does not match the mathematical precision of a Roman temple.</meta>

# STONEHENGE

<margin><term>Table XVII</term></margin> The best view of this sort, to be had from our work, is from the north, as shown in <term>Table XVII.</term> <note>original: "TAB. XVII."</note> The reader cannot help but observe how little pretense there is here for an imitation of *Greek* or *Roman* porticos, notwithstanding the grand and agreeable curve of the outer circle. But when we see the disproportion of the inner circle in regard to any purpose of this sort, we must admit that the invention of <term>Hermogenes</term> <note>A famous Greek architect of the 2nd century BCE known for his strict rules on symmetry.</note> in contriving the <term>pseudo-dipteros</term> <note>A temple design that looks like it has a double row of columns but has actually removed the inner row to save space.</note> is applied here with very little grace. The founders of <term>Stonehenge</term> could have no need for makeshifts for want of room on <term>Salisbury Plain</term>. Or how could a concentric row of little <term>stones</term>—or pillars, if he will have it so—bear any resemblance to the contrivance of <term>Hermogenes</term>, which consisted in having none; that is, in taking away the whole inner row of pillars so as to add to the convenience of space while preserving the appearance at the same time? Most undoubtedly, the <term>Druids</term> had no further meaning in it than to make use of the even numbers of 30 greater <term>stones</term> and 40 lesser <term>stones</term>; and this was to produce a more complex variety, as the gaps between them have no relationship to one another. They were so far from having a notion of *Grecian* beauty, where the pillars of circular porticos are set on the same radius—pillar answering to pillar, and the space between columns answering to the next. And this will be shown repeatedly in the progress of this work to be the common practice of the <term>Druids</term> in other similar instances.

<margin><term>Table XVIII</term></margin> But when we consider the "cell," as Mr. *Webb* <note>John Webb, the architect who argued Stonehenge was a Roman temple built for the god Coelus.</note> names it, we find him guilty of great dishonesty in poorly conceiving its form and in distorting his floor plans to better color his argument. The minute you enter this <term>adytum</term> <note>The innermost, most sacred part of a temple; original: "adytum."</note>, as shown in <term>Table XVIII</term>, you discover it is not a hexagon, nor was it ever intended for one, and there can be no greater absurdity than to imagine it as such. It is, in truth, composed of certain joined structures of <term>stones</term>, which I shall call <term>trilithons</term> <note>From the Greek for "three stones"; a structure consisting of two large vertical stones supporting a third horizontal stone across the top.</note>, because each is made of two upright <term>stones</term> with an impost <note>A horizontal stone or beam.</note> at the top. There are clearly five of these <term>trilithons</term> remaining. But the naked eye easily discovers that they are very far from making five sides of a hexagon. They cannot be brought into any alignment that resembles a truly circular polygon. Three <term>trilithons</term> of the five remain entire; two are indeed ruined to some extent, but the <term>stones</term> remain in their original place <note>original: "in situ"</note>. And nothing is easier than to reconstruct the floor plan from symmetry and correspondence. We see the two <term>trilithons</term> on the wings or sides of the <term>adytum</term> are set almost in a straight line with one another; whereas in a hexagon form, they ought to make a considerable angle. If you examine this mathematically, the true angle of a hexagon is 120 degrees, but here there is an angle of nearly 150. By making it a hexagon, Webb supposes one <term>trilithon</term> is entirely gone—the one nearest the grand entrance—when there is not the least appearance that such <term>stones</term> were ever there. There is no cavity in the earth, no stump or fragment visible, nor is it easy to imagine how three <term>stones</term> of such vast bulk could have been carried away cleanly, either whole or in pieces. There is no room for them to have been carried away whole, and no traces of them having been thrown down, broken, and then carried away. This outer side of the work is actually the most perfect part of the whole. Regarding the ruins of the other <term>trilithons</term>, not the smallest part is missing. What has been thrown down and broken remains on the spot. But this disputed <term>trilithon</term> must have been spirited away by nothing less than <term>Merlin's magic</term> <note>A reference to the medieval legend that Merlin the wizard moved the stones from Ireland to Salisbury Plain.</note>, which the monks' fables say erected it. Besides, even if it were still standing, it would be very far from making this <term>adytum</term> a regular hexagon, which he used to support his <term>peripteros</term> <note>A temple surrounded by a single row of columns.</note> scheme on page 87. Furthermore, even granting it was a regular hexagon, it would be very far from corresponding with that scheme or having the least appearance of being taken from one. For our editor there has converted the "cell" into something quite different from the nature of the one at <term>Stonehenge</term>. He has drawn the upper end of his cell at the letter H (opposite the grand entrance G) not as a <term>trilithon</term>, as it notoriously is at <term>Stonehenge</term>, but as an angular gap between two <term>trilithons</term>. In his version, it is an angle rather than a side of the figure. Whereas it is most obvious at <term>Stonehenge</term> that the upper end of

<summary>The author refutes John Webb’s claim that Stonehenge was a Roman temple, using mathematical measurements and site observations to prove that the inner sanctuary (adytum) is not a regular hexagon. He highlights that the stones do not follow classical Greek or Roman rules of symmetry and ridicules the idea that a "missing" stone was magically removed.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of John Webb’s architectural theories regarding Stonehenge, specifically focusing on the alignment of the inner sanctuary. It transitions into Chapter V, which compares the site to ancient Greek and Syrian structures.</meta>

->D E S C R I B E D .<-

of the *innermost sanctuary* <note>original: "adytum," the most sacred inner chamber of a temple.</note> opposite to the grand entrance, and to the whole length of the avenue and entrance between it and the *central space*, is a <term>trilithon (a structure consisting of two upright stones supporting a third horizontal lintel)</term>; not an angle or interval. And that *trilithon* is exceeding stately, though in ruins, one of the upright stones being fallen, the other leaning. So that here, we have the cell converted full a sixth part of the whole compass, from its true and original situation, and so in all the schemes of *Mr. Webb’s* book <note>referring to John Webb’s 1655 publication of Inigo Jones’s notes, which argued Stonehenge was a Roman temple.</note>, not one excepted. In that, for instance, *Scheme I, p. 56*, the high altar is placed at D, not against a *trilithon*, as it ought to be, opposite to the grand entrance in the front of the temple, and to the (only) entrance below, into the *area*, but against an angle between two. If then you suppose that hexagon removed back a sixth part, so as that a *trilithon* be set behind the high altar, as it is really in the thing itself, and upon the principal diameter of the whole work: then this absurd consequence follows, that the opposite *trilithon* of the cell stands in the very midst of the entrance into the cell, upon the same principal ground-line or diameter of the work, and quite obstructs the view and entrance into it. It is altogether as ridiculous as if a dead wall was built under *St. Paul’s* organ-loft, which is and ought to be the chief entrance into the choir. Besides, by *Webb’s* ground-plots and elevations, it seems as if, when you entered this *innermost sanctuary*, there were 3 *trilithons* on the right, and 3 on the left, whereas it is most obvious, there are but two on the right, and two on the left; when you advance into it, the orderly way, from the north-east grand entrance of the avenue; which he himself on page 55 owns to be the principal. But I am tired of so ungrateful a task, which necessity alone could have extorted from me.

## CHAP. V.

### Of the cell or innermost sanctuary of Stonehenge. Of the Surgeons' amphitheater, London.

<margin>TABLE XVIII.</margin>
Disputations become cloisters and porticoes. Let us now with minds free from passion, enter the *innermost sanctuary* with an intent to find out its true figure, to examine what it really was, and what it is. And that may easily be done because (as I said before) as to the *trilithons* of which it is chiefly composed, they are all remaining. Not a bit is lost, but what mischievous and silly people knock off with hammers, to see whether, as the wretched vulgar notion would have it, the stones be artificial <note>original: "factitious." At the time, there was a popular but incorrect theory that the massive stones were a form of ancient concrete or man-made casting rather than natural rock.</note>. TABLE XVIII. is a design of it, which I made sitting in the center of the grand entrance in the inner circle. This point is properly the door-way or entrance into the *innermost sanctuary*, as a wicket or little door, whilst the doorposts of the nearest *trilithons* present themselves, as the greater door, of above 40 feet wide, or 25 cubits. I observe in the old *Greek* story, many footsteps of the primitive patriarchal way left in their sacred structures, which are parallels to this work before us, and others of our *Druids*. For instance, *Pausanias in his description of Attica* <note>original: "Pausanias in atticis," referring to the 2nd-century AD Greek traveler's writings.</note> speaks of a temple dedicated to *Venus*, in the front of which, is a wall (as he calls it) built of rude stones. Nevertheless he concludes it to be a very famous work. One may very well imagine, this wall of rude stones is the remnant of some such old work as ours, left for the sacred regard the people had to it, even after art was risen to great height, together with superstition and idolatry. For that the most ancient *Greeks* had very little of idolatry, any more than our *Druids*, I shall show when I discourse on that head. Again: the more sacred part of the temple at *Hierapolis* <note>An ancient city in modern-day Turkey, known for its sacred hot springs.</note> answering to our *innermost sanctuary*, had no door, though none entered therein but the chief priests. *Lucian in "On the Syrian Goddess"* <note>original: "Lucian de deâ Syria."</note>. I suppose it was in imitation of the ancient usage, without doors to shut or open, as our temple here. For the ancients thought it wrong, to confine the deity, as it

were, within any covered place: till *Moses*, by God's direction, made a tabernacle covered with skins, which was to foreshadow <note>original: "adumbrate"</note> the Messiah Son of God, who was to be clothed with our nature. And *Solomon's* temple was built in imitation of this tabernacle. But before that, the ancients meant no more by temples, or altars, as they were first called, than a certain known and conspicuous place, ornamented in a particular manner, that should mark out a <term>kebla</term> <note>The direction towards which a worshiper faces; usually refers to the direction of Mecca in Islam, but here used generally for any sacred orientation.</note>, or a place towards which we are to address the Deity, and that for uniformity's sake. As the *Turks* and *Arabians* do now, who are the descendants of *Ishmael*, and had this custom from *Abraham*. Though the supreme Being be omnipresent, yet for our convenience, where time, place, and such kind of circumstances are necessary to a public action, he would have, as it were, the place of his presence made notorious. As in the *Jewish* dispensation he did in a most extraordinary manner, by the <term>Shechinah</term> <note>The visible manifestation of the glory of God.</note>. And from *Solomon's* temple, all the rest of the world borrowed the fashion of temples, properly so called, built magnificently and with roofs. For the sacred houses mentioned in scripture before then, were only little chapels, shrines, like our Druids’ <term>kistvaens</term> <note>A type of stone-lined burial chamber or chest, from the Welsh "cist" (chest) and "maen" (stone).</note>, which sometimes they carried about in a cart, sometimes were fixed in cities, for public use; as *Beth Dagon* <note>The "House of Dagon," a Philistine temple.</note>, and the like. These were but <term>kistvaens</term> improved, niches turned into small shrines <note>original: "sacella"</note>, in imitation of two or three stones in *Abraham's* altars, which we may well call the <term>kebla</term>, and find many of them among our Druid antiquities.

The cell is formed by a radius of 12 cubits and a half, from the two centers *a* and *b*, as to the inward curve; the outward takes a radius of 15 cubits; for these stones are two cubits and a half thick. The two circles are turned into an oval, by a radius of 30 cubits, (after the usual manner) set in the two centers *c* and *d*, where the two circles intersect. The former centers are 12 cubits and a half distant from each other, the length of the radius. The same oval is obtained by a string of 60 cubits, the ends tied together, and turned round upon two centers, according to the gardener's method. An oval formed as this is, upon two centers coinciding with each other's circumference; or, which is the same thing, whose centers are distant from each other the length of their radius, is most natural and most beautiful, being the shape of an egg. Most probably these religious philosophers had a meaning, in thus including an egg-like figure, within a circle, more than mere affectation of variety. Whatever that was, we may reasonably conclude, that from the method in antiquity, of making the <term>kebla</term> of a curved figure, the Christians borrowed theirs of turning the east end of their churches in that manner; and that the Druids in the work before us, have produced the noblest <term>kistvaen</term> or <term>kebla</term> that is known.

My purpose in drawing many dotted <note>original: "prickt"</note> lines upon the plate, is not difficult to be understood. Nor does it require particular explanations. To avoid affectation or tediousness, I leave them to the reader's amusement: only observe, that *Mr. Webb's* equilateral triangles have no hand in forming the cell. The intent of it is very distant from a regular polygon. But that it is incomparably more beautiful than such a one would have rendered it. It is as a magnificent niche 27 cubits long, and as much broad, measuring in the widest place.

This part is called the inner shrine <note>original Greek: "Σηκος" (sekos)</note> or temple shell <note>original: "concha templi"</note> and <term>adytum</term> <note>The innermost sanctuary or "holy of holies" of a temple.</note>, into which, we may suppose, none but the upper order of priests, together with the high-priest, were commonly to enter, during the time of ministration, in religious rites. We may imagine the beauty of the appearance here upon those occasions, when an innumerable company of the Druids assisted, all in white surplices. The center of the eccentricity of this oval is but three cubits nearer the entrance, than the center of the whole work. And they have cut off but one <term>trilithon</term> <note>A structure consisting of two large vertical stones supporting a third stone set horizontally across the top.</note>, which they make the opening of the <term>adytum</term>; meeting the eye to great advantage, from the grand entrance. By the aforesaid contrivance, there is left a space of five cubits between the jambs

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual and geometric design of Stonehenge's inner cell, arguing that its oval shape served as a 'kebla' (focal point for prayer) similar to ancient Hebrew and Middle Eastern traditions. The author describes the mathematical proportions of the oval and critiques previous Roman-centric theories of its design.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the inner sanctuary of Stonehenge, following the discussion of its circular and elliptical proportions.</meta>

## DESCRIBED.

The doorposts of the opening of the <term>adytum</term> <note>the innermost, most sacred part of a temple</note>, and the inner circle in front, are arranged exactly as the space between the inner and outer circles. The inner circle there performs the function of <term>cancelli</term> <note>original Latin: latticed screens or railings that separate a choir or altar from the main body of a church</note> to it, as we observed before. If a choir of this form were put into practice and built by a master’s hand, it would have a very extraordinary effect, and perhaps even excel the similar hollow curve of a <term>cupola</term> <note>a dome-like structure on a roof</note>. Our <term>Druids</term> <note>ancient Celtic priests and learned elite</note> undoubtedly had such a concept in mind when they placed this within a circle. To achieve this, they turned the two circles into a smaller kind of <term>ellipsis</term> <note>an oval shape</note>.

There is a <term>Druid</term> antiquity similar in shape to our <term>adytum</term> called *Eglwys Glominog* <note>Welsh: likely "Church of the Dove"</note>, located on the top of *Arennig vaur* in *Lhanykil parish, Merionydhshire*, but it is made of a continuous wall. The ancients believed the world was shaped like an egg, and since the world is the temple of the Deity, they judged it proper to form their temples to resemble it. The ancient hieroglyphic for the Deity is a circle, and I have reason to believe this symbol is more ancient than the Great Flood. *Plato*, who learned much from the ancestors of our <term>Druids</term>, says in the writings of *Diogenes Laertius* that "God is spherical," which he must have meant symbolically. Thus, our <term>Druids</term>, as well as he, may signify the infinity of nature in the Deity who created the world through this design of <term>Stonehenge</term>; at the very least, they understand the circle to represent the seat and residence of the Deity—the heavens, which encompass all things.

It seems to me that the architect *Inigo Jones* used this <term>adytum</term> as a model for the plan of the <term>Surgeons' theatre</term> in *London*, a building designed for seeing and hearing that is much admired by all skilled judges. My Lord *Burlington*, out of a truly noble spirit and a great love for the architect’s memory, has lately repaired it at his own expense and with his own excellent skill. I find that the <term>Surgeons' theatre</term> (or rather <term>amphitheatre</term>) is formed using the same proportions as our <term>adytum</term>, with the long and short diameters being at a ratio of 4 to 3—namely, 40 feet and 30 feet. This appears to me a strong piece of evidence that *Inigo Jones* did not actually create the ground plan of <term>Stonehenge</term> that was published under his name. The <term>Surgeons' amphitheatre</term> is considerably smaller than our central cell.

Such is the noble and simple geometry of the <term>adytum</term> of <term>Stonehenge</term>. The stones that compose it are truly stupendous; their height, breadth, and thickness are enormous. To see so many of them placed together in a precise and carefully constructed shape creates a sense of awe in the mind that words cannot express. One very remarkable detail in the construction of this <term>adytum</term> has escaped all previous observers: this part is composed of <term>trilithons</term> <note>structures consisting of two large vertical stones supporting a third horizontal stone (the lintel) across the top</note> set two by two on each side, with one directly in front. These stones increase in height and beauty from the lower end of the <term>adytum</term> to the upper end. By this, I mean that the two nearest <term>trilithons</term>—those next to the grand entrance on the right and left—are shorter than the next two in the sequence; and those, in turn, are shorter than the <term>trilithon</term> behind the altar at the upper end of this choir. Therefore, in recording the measurements of the parts that make up this place, the reader must be willing to take my word for it. Mr. *Webb*'s <note>John Webb, who edited and published Inigo Jones's notes on Stonehenge</note> measurements cannot be precise, as he knew nothing of this particular detail, and his idea that the layout was a <term>hexagon</term> <note>a six-sided shape</note> is contradicted by this fact as well as by physical evidence. "He says on page 60, the stones of the greater <term>hexagon</term> are seven feet and a half in breadth, three feet nine inches thick, and twenty-seven feet high, each stone having one tenon in the middle." His measurement of breadth only shows the vastness of the stones; it is not a precise measurement, for the builders did not care for exactness in their breadth. This is because two stones together were designed to make a <term>compages</term> <note>original Latin: a structure or joining together</note> upon which to set the <term>impost</term> <note>the horizontal top stone of a trilithon</note>, and this I call a <term>trilithon</term>. Each <term>trilithon</term> stands by itself, independent of its neighbor—unlike the stones and <term>imposts</term> of the outer circle, which are linked together in a continuous <term>corona</term> <note>original Latin: a crown or continuous ring</note> by the <term>imposts</term> carried all the way around. Indeed, the breadth of a stone at the bottom is seven feet and a
