# STONEHENGE

half, which is 4 cubits and a half. Two stones therefore amount to nine cubits, and there is a cubit of interval between them, making in the whole ten cubits. But they were not careful of the particulars, only of the whole, in one of these *joinings* <note>original Latin: "compages," referring to a structural assembly</note> or *trilithons* <note>a structure consisting of two large vertical stones supporting a third stone set horizontally across the top</note>.

<margin>Plate XV</margin>
The stones of the cell are made to diminish very much towards the top, most apparently with a design to take off from their weight, and render them what we call top-heavy in a lesser degree. Hence the interval between the two upright stones of the *joining* <note>original Latin: "compages"</note> widens so much upwards. This must certainly contribute very much to their stability. In assigning 20 feet for their height, Mr. *Webb* <note>John Webb (1611–1672), an architect who published an early study of Stonehenge</note> has well taken the *average* <note>original: "medium"</note>. A very small matter more than 20 feet makes exactly 12 cubits of the *Hebrews*, *Egyptians* and *Druids*. The reader remembers the proportion I assigned between the *English* foot and this cubit. 20 inches and 4/5 make a cubit, therefore 20 feet and 4/5 make 12 cubits. The true case as to the height of the *trilithons* is thus respectively, and which may be seen in Plate XV, with the harmony and symmetry in the proportion of the whole. We may observe their gradual rising in height, all from the same base, like pillars of higher orders and more diameters. But the intelligent reader must needs see that our founders never had sight of *Greek* or *Roman* pillars, and never pretended to imitate them, or take any one idea from them. And of these three different orders or degrees of altitude, in these *trilithons*, one exceeds the other by a cubit. So that their heights respectively are 13 cubits, 14 cubits, 15 cubits.

The **imposts** <note>the horizontal top stones, or lintels, that rest on the uprights</note> of these *trilithons* are all of the same height. Mr. *Webb* page 61, "informs us, the **architrave** <note>the main beam resting across the tops of the pillars</note> lying on the top of the great stones of the hexagon and mortised also into them [is] sixteen feet long, 3 feet 9 inches broad, 3 feet 4 inches high." Mr. *Webb*’s 16 feet long is too scanty, it amounting to 9 cubits and 2 palms, but the intent of the founders was to make these **imposts** equal both in length and breadth to the foundation of the upright stones that supports them—I mean the two stones at bottom, the sustaining part of the *joining* <note>original Latin: "compages"</note>, which in its whole breadth makes 10 cubits; and 10 cubits long the **imposts** are to be assigned. Most certainly whoever undertakes to measure them, whether from those fallen on the ground or still in their proper place, will be apt to fail in giving them just length. Both because 1. it is observable that these **imposts** are formed somewhat broader upwards than in their bottom part; but this may not be taken notice of by every one. This was done very judiciously upon an optical principle, which it is plain the founders were aware of. For a stone of so considerable an elevation, by this means only, presents its whole face in view. Therefore they that measure it at bottom will not take its true length. 2. If they take the dimension, either from a stone still in its proper place, or from one fallen down, they will be very liable to shorten the measure. For in the first case, the upper edge of these **imposts** must needs have suffered from the weather, in so elevated an exposure, through the space of 2000 years. It is very apparent they have suffered not a little. Large and deep furrows of age are visible all around them. But if they measure those fallen, they must well imagine such have doubly suffered, from weather, and from the people every day diminishing all corners and edges to carry pieces away with them. So that in this case, analogy and symmetry only can supply these defects. Thus we found before, that the breadth of the **imposts** of the outer circle is equal to their <term>ichnographical</term> <note>pertaining to a ground plan or horizontal section of a building</note> breadth: so it is here, being 10 cubits. Besides, the outer face of these **imposts** is longer than the inner, as being in the larger circle. Therefore ten cubits is to be understood as their *average* <note>original: "medium"</note> measure.

Mr. *Webb* gives it as a general measure, that they are 3 feet 9 inches broad. He has before told us, the uprights which support them were 3 feet 9 [inches] thick; take that twice, it makes 7 feet and a half, which he assigns for the breadth of the uprights. This is all just within a trifle, and it is

### DESCRIBED.

It is not expected that he who was not aware of the <term>cubit</term> <note>an ancient unit of length based on the forearm, approximately 18 to 21 inches</note>, by which these works were made, should do it with greater accuracy. The truth of the whole is this: *Webb's* <note>John Webb (1611–1672), an architect who published early surveys of Stonehenge</note> 7 foot and half is 4 <term>cubits</term> and a half, as we said before; the half of it is 3 foot 9, and a very little more. But this must be taken for the least breadth of the <term>imposts</term> <note>the horizontal capstones or lintels resting on the upright stones</note>, that at the ends. For in the middle they are somewhat broader. Though the inside faces are straight, yet, as we observed, in the proper place, of the <term>imposts</term> of the outer circle; so here, they are rounded behind: their outer circumference answering to the great oval upon which they are founded. So likewise their ends are made upon a <term>radius</term> <note>a line from the center to the edge of a circle or oval</note> of that oval, whence the inner face of the <term>impost</term> is somewhat shorter than the outer, and is another reason why their lengths may easily be taken somewhat too short. I have drawn the <term>imposts</term> in their true shape in the ground-plot. The artifice of the <term>tenons</term> <note>a projecting piece of stone intended to fit into a hole</note> and <term>mortises</term> <note>a hole or groove cut into a stone to receive a tenon</note> of these <term>trilithons</term> <note>a structure consisting of two large vertical stones supporting a third horizontal stone across the top</note> and their <term>imposts</term>, what conformity they bear to that of the outer circle, is exceedingly pretty, everything being done truly geometrical, and as would best answer every purpose, from plain and simple principles. In the bottom face of the <term>impost</term>, if divided into three squares, the two <term>mortises</term> are made in the middle of the two outermost squares. Draw diagonal lines from corner to corner; where they intersect, is the center of the <term>mortise</term>; which central distance from one to the other, is seven <term>cubits</term> of the <term>Druid measure</term>. Each <term>tenon</term> is a <term>cubit</term> broad upon its longest diameter, for they are of an oval figure. An admirable contrivance, that the <term>imposts</term> should lie firm upon the heads of the uprights, and keep the uprights steady in their places, to strengthen and adorn. We may remark this pretty device, in the management of the <term>tenons</term> and <term>mortises</term>. Cut an egg across upon its shortest diameter or conjugate; one half thereof represents the shape of the <term>tenons</term> of the outer circle. Cut it across upon its transverse diameter, one half is the shape of the <term>tenons</term> of the <term>adytum</term> <note>the innermost sanctuary or shrine of the temple</note>. It is evident the meaning of it is this: The <term>tenons</term> of the outer circle are higher in proportion than the others, because the <term>imposts</term> are less and lower than the others, and on both accounts more liable to be disturbed, either by accident or violence, than the others: therefore more caution is used for their preservation. This is an instance of art, noble and simple withal. Mr. *Webb* says the <term>imposts</term> are 3 foot 4 inches high, which is precisely 2 <term>cubits</term>, a sixth part of the height of the middle order of <term>trilithons</term>; as the <term>imposts</term> of the outer circle are a sixth part of the height of the stones of the outer circle. The middle order of <term>trilithons</term> is above 24 foot high, that is, 14 <term>cubits</term>. The lower order is 13 <term>cubits</term>, namely, those next the entrance. The upper <term>trilithon</term> behind the altar was 15 <term>cubits</term>. Each rising a <term>cubit</term> higher than the other, as we before observed.

The one shows the front of the temple when in perfection, the other as now in ruins. The other and most perfect side of the <term>adytum</term> <note>from the Greek word for 'not to be entered,' referring here to the innermost sacred sanctuary of the monument</note>. It is an oblique prospect of it, viewed from the entrance.

The quantity of solid material is well adjusted in the proportioning of the stonework of this inner sanctuary to the intervals on the <term>ichnography</term> <note>a ground plan or map showing the relative positions of the parts of a building</note>. Each <term>trilithon</term> <note>a structure consisting of two large vertical stones supporting a third horizontal stone across the top</note> is 10 <term>cubits</term> <note>an ancient unit of length, approximately 20.8 inches in this context</note>, and each interval is about 6. The jambs, or the <term>vacuum</term> <note>the empty space or opening</note> of the entry, expand themselves to 25 cubits, which is about 43 feet. From this measure, my Lord *Pembroke* demonstrated the falsity of *Webb's* <note>John Webb (1611–1672), an architect who incorrectly argued Stonehenge was a Roman temple built on a hexagonal plan</note> hexagonal scheme when his Lordship first did me the honor of discussing *Stonehenge*. In Mr. *Webb's* designs, we find two jambs (taking one trilithon away) expand but little above 31 feet, by his own scales. Though I do not pretend that some of my previous measures might not vary a little here and there upon a very strict trial, or where proper judgment is not used—because the stones in some parts may bulge out, or large pieces of them may have fallen off—yet a 10-foot difference from the truth can-

<margin>
**PLATE XIX, XX.**
</margin>

not be allowed. In Plates XIX and XX, observe the inside of that upright stone which makes the northern jamb of the chief entrance of the outer circle. A very large piece has fallen off toward the top, which reveals its <term>tenon</term> <note>a projecting "peg" carved into the top of the upright stone</note> and the <term>mortise</term> <note>the hole or socket carved into the underside of the lintel to receive the tenon</note> of the <term>impost</term> <note>the horizontal lintel stone</note> above it. In the management of such prodigious stones as these, fixed in the ground and rammed in like posts, it is not to be wondered at if we find some small variations by chance. Though for my own part, I observed none; rather, I wondered how it was possible for them, without <term>lewices</term> <note>iron lifting tools inserted into a hole in the stone to raise it</note> and similar devices, to set them in their places with such precision. 

The reader, whose mind has received no previous bias, cannot but be abundantly satisfied that the multitude of measures I have given from Mr. *Webb's* own account are perfectly agreeable to the scale of cubits deduced from the works of the *Egyptians* and others—and that in round and full numbers, not trifling fractions. If we compare the numbers given with the *Roman* scale, the measures appear very ridiculous and without design; and that is a sure way of disproving the opinion that this is a *Roman* work. But as these stones are generally rough, and must suffer in all dimensions over time, it is not practical to take their true measure without necessary judgment and a consideration of symmetry.

Of these greater stones of the sanctuary, as I observed before, none are missing. They are all on the spot: 10 upright stones and 5 <term>cornices</term> <note>the horizontal lintel stones or imposts</note>. The trilithon first on the left hand is entire in its original position, but vastly decayed, especially the cornice. There are such deep holes corroded in some places that <note>original: "daws"</note> jackdaws make their nests in them. The next trilithon on the left hand is entire, composed of three most beautiful stones. The cornice happened to be of a very durable kind of *English* marble and has not been much impaired by weather. My Lord *Winchelsea* and myself took a considerable walk on the top of it, but it was a frightening situation. The trilithon at the upper end of the sanctuary was of extraordinary beauty. But alas, probably through the indiscretion of someone digging there between them and the altar, the noble horizontal stone has been dislodged from its airy seat and has fallen upon the altar, where its huge bulk lies unbroken.

> After a long time, the weight fell back onto the solid earth, and displayed art joined with strength. <note>original: "Recidit in solidam longo post tempore, terram / Pondus, et exhibuit junctam cum viribus artem."</note> ->*Ovid Metamorphoses*<-

The two uprights that supported it are the most delicate stones of the whole work. They were, I believe, above 30 feet long, and well-chiseled, finely tapered and proportioned in their dimensions. That on the southern side is broken in two, lying upon the altar. The other still stands entire but leans upon one of the stones of the inward oval.

# DESCRIBED.

I

<meta>catchword: CHAPTER</meta>

The root-end or unworked part of both stones are raised somewhat above the ground. We cannot be certain of the true height of this structure when it was perfect, but I am sure the 15 <term>cubits</term> <note>An ancient unit of measurement, approximately 18 to 20 inches or 45 to 50 cm.</note> which I have assigned to it is the lowest estimate. The next <term>trilithon</term> <note>A prehistoric structure consisting of two large vertical stones supporting a third stone set horizontally across the top (a lintel).</note>, the one toward the west, is intact, except that some of the end of the <term>impost</term> <note>The horizontal lintel stone resting on top of the two uprights.</note> has fallen clean off, and the entire upper edge is very much worn away by time. As the Roman poet Lucretius says:

->*We see that every thing wanes, and we perceive all things flow away as if by long-enduring time, and age withdraws them from our sight.* <note>original Latin: "Minui rem quamque videmus, / Et quasi longinquo fluere omnia cernimus ævo, / Ex oculisque, vetustatem, subducere nostris." from Lucretius's *On the Nature of Things*.</note><-

The last <term>trilithon</term>, the one on the right hand of the entrance into the <term>adytum</term> <note>The innermost sanctuary or "holy of holies" of the temple structure.</note>, has suffered much damage. The outer upright stone, being the side post of the entrance, is still standing; the other upright and the horizontal stone are both fallen forward into the sanctuary and broke into three pieces each. I suppose this happened from people digging near it. However, because one piece of the horizontal stone is lying loose in the middle between the side posts of the sanctuary, Mr. Webb <note>John Webb (1611–1672), an architect who published a book claiming Stonehenge was a Roman temple.</note>, in the plan of his ruins of <term>Stonehenge</term>, uses this single stone to create an imaginary sixth <term>trilithon</term>. He assumes it was one of the stones of the inner or lesser <term>hexagon</term>, as he calls it. Yet, if this fragment were really the stump of such a stone as he claims, it would not create a hexagonal shape for the cell; instead, it would stand right in the middle of the entrance and block it up in a very absurd, unseemly, and inconvenient manner. Nothing can be more certain than that such a structure never existed. The stone of the <term>trilithon</term> that is still standing has a cavity in it, worn by the weather, large enough for two or three people to sit in.

<margin>PLATE XV.</margin>

<term>Stonehenge</term> is composed of two circles and two ovals, sharing the same center. At a distance of two <term>cubits</term> inward from the larger oval, draw another smaller oval on which the stones of the inner oval are to stand. These are 19 stones in number, spaced about 3 <term>cubits</term> apart from their centers. This smaller oval is to be drawn using a string and two center points, *a* and *b*, just as the other circles were drawn from a 10 <term>cubit</term> radius, as was mentioned before. Mr. Webb says on page 60, “the stones of the <term>hexagon</term> within are 2 feet 6 inches in breadth, one foot and a half thick, and 8 feet high, in a pyramidal form.” His "two feet and a half" is the same as our <term>cubit</term> and a half for the width of these stones—being only a third of the width of the stones in the larger oval. The gap between each stone is the same. Their height is also unequal, like the <term>trilithons</term>, for they increase in height as they get closer to the upper end of the sanctuary. Mr. Webb’s assigned height of 8 feet is a good average measure, for it is exactly 4 <term>cubits</term> and 4 <term>palms</term> <note>A smaller unit of measurement, about 3 inches or 7.5 cm.</note>, which is one-third the height of the average <term>trilithon</term>. From the ruins of those left, we may well suppose the first stones next to the entrance and the lowest ones were 4 <term>cubits</term> high; the tallest height behind the altar might have been five <term>cubits</term>, and perhaps more. The stones are somewhat like what Mr. Webb calls a "pyramidal form," meaning they resemble an <term>Egyptian obelisk</term> <note>A tall, four-sided, tapering monument which ends in a pyramid-like shape at the top.</note> because they taper slightly toward the top. They are made of a much harder type of stone than the others, as was mentioned regarding the smaller circle. The builders ensured that their smaller size was made up for by their density. They were brought from somewhere in the west. Of these, only six remain standing upright. The stumps of two are left on the south side by the altar. One lies behind the altar, either dug up or knocked down by the fall of the upright stone there. One or two were likely knocked down by the fall of the upright of the first <term>trilithon</term> on the right hand. A stump of another remains by the upright there, which is still standing. Their exact measurements—height, width, or thickness—cannot be easily determined because the builders used stones they could find that best suited their needs, but the stones were better shaped and taller as they approached the upper end of the cell.

The author refutes John Webb’s theory of a hexagonal layout, arguing instead for a more practical and aesthetically consistent oval design.</summary>

## STONEHENGE
### CHAPTER VI.

*Concerning the number of the stones. Concerning the altar-stone. Concerning what has been found during excavation around the temple. A tin plate featuring Druidic writing. A gold plate, believed to feature Druidic writing.*

THUS we have finished our description of the work, or the primary portion of this famous wonder; what may properly be called the temple or sacred structure. Even though its highest point is composed merely of one stone laid upon another. “A work,” as Mr. Webb <note>John Webb (1611–1672), an architect who published Inigo Jones’s notes on Stonehenge, arguing it was a Roman temple.</note> rightly says on page 65, “built with much art, order, and proportion; and it must be admitted that those who believed it was unworthy to attempt to confine the deity within rooms and walls could not easily invent a grander design than this for sacred purposes, nor execute it in a more magnificent manner.” Here, space is indeed marked out and defined, but with the greatest freedom and openness. Here is a <term>kebbla</term> <note>A term borrowed from the Arabic 'Qibla,' referring to the direction or focal point of prayer; the author uses it here to describe the central focus of the temple.</note> suggesting, but not enclosing, the presence of the Deity. Here, the variety and harmony of four different circles presents itself as something continually new with every step we take, through the shifting patterns of light and shade. Whichever way we look, art and nature combine with the highest taste to create a pleasing astonishment, very appropriate for sacred places.

The great oval consists of 10 uprights; the inner circle with the altar consists of 20; the great circle consists of 30; and the inner circle consists of 40. 10, 20, 30, and 40 together make 100 upright stones. There are 5 <term>imposts</term> <note>Horizontal lintel stones spanning the tops of upright stones.</note> on the great oval, 30 on the great circle, 2 stones standing upon the bank of the <term>area</term> <note>The level space enclosed by the earthworks.</note>, the stone lying within the entrance of the <term>area</term>, and the stone standing with another stone lying upon the ground by the <term>vallum</term> <note>An earthen wall or rampart.</note> of the court, directly opposite the entrance of the avenue. All added together, these make exactly 140 stones, the total number of which <term>Stonehenge</term>, as a complete temple, is composed. Behold the solution to the great problem; the magical spell is broken which has so long puzzled the common people! They believe it is an unlucky thing to count the true number of the stones, and that whoever does so shall certainly die afterward. In this way, the <term>Druids</term> <note>The priestly class of the ancient Celts, whom the author credits with building Stonehenge.</note> were content to live in huts and <term>caves</term>, while they employed many thousands of men—an entire county's worth of labor—to work on these public structures dedicated to the Deity.

Our altar here is placed toward the upper end of the <term>adytum</term> <note>The innermost sanctuary or "holy of holies."</note>; at present it lies flat on the ground and is squeezed (as it were) into it by the weight of the ruins fallen upon it. It is a kind of blue coarse marble, such as comes from <term>Derbyshire</term> <note>While the author identifies it as Derbyshire marble, modern geology identifies the "Altar Stone" as a specific type of green micaceous sandstone from South Wales.</note>, and which is laid upon tombs in our churches and churchyards. Thus <term>Virgil</term> describes an ancient altar, after the <term>Etruscan</term> fashion, which had probably remained from the times of the patriarchs:

> *In the center of the palace, under the naked axis of the sky,*
> *stood a massive altar.*
> <note>original Latin: "Ædibus in mediis nudoque sub ætheris axe / Ingens ara fuit." from Virgil's Æneid, Book II.</note>
->Æneid II.<-

<term>Servius</term> <note>A 4th-century grammarian known for his commentaries on Virgil.</note>, commenting upon the third book of the *Georgics*, says that in the middle of a temple was the place of the Deity; the rest was only ornamental. This altar is placed a little above the <term>focus</term> of the upper end of the <term>ellipsis</term> <note>The geometric center or focal point of the oval arrangement.</note>. <term>Mr. Webb</term> says on page 56 that the altar is 4 feet broad and 16 feet in length. 4 feet is 2 <term>cubits</term> and 2 <term>palms</term> <note>The author uses a "Druid's cubit" of approximately 20.8 inches. A palm is a smaller unit, usually a fraction of a cubit.</note>, which, when multiplied four times, measures 16 feet. I believe its breadth is 2 <term>cubits</term> and 3 <term>palms</term> (that is, 1 and a half <term>cubits</term>), and that its originally intended length was 10 <term>cubits</term>, equal to the breadth of the <term>trilithon</term> <note>A structure consisting of two upright stones and a capping lintel.</note> before which it lies. However, it is very difficult to determine its true length. It is 20 inches thick, exactly one <term>cubit</term>, and has been squared. It lies between the two centers—that established by the <term>compasses</term> and that established by the <term>string</term> <note>Surveying techniques used to layout the circular and oval designs of the site.</note>—leaving a convenient space all around it, no doubt as much as was necessary for the priests' ministry.

### DESCRIBED.

Mr. Webb says the heads of oxen, deer, and other beasts have been found while digging in and around **Stonehenge**. Several people living at the time could testify to this, and they are undoubtedly the relics of sacrifices, found alongside much charcoal (meaning wood-ashes). Mr. **Camden** says human bones have been found in this area as well. He refers to the nearby barrows <note>ancient burial mounds</note>, and I personally saw such bones thrown out of the earth by rabbits very near the temple itself. 

However, we must eternally lament the loss of a certain tin tablet found at this site during the reign of **King Henry VIII** (the era of the restoration of learning and pure religion). It was inscribed with many letters, but in such a strange character that neither **Sir Thomas Elliot**, a learned antiquary, nor Mr. **Lilly**, the headmaster of St. Paul’s School, could make any sense of it. Mr. **Sammes** may be correct in judging it to have been **Punic** <note>The language of ancient Carthage</note>; I imagine if we call it **Irish** <note>Referring to ancient Gaelic or Ogham script</note>, we shall not be far from the truth. Without a doubt, it was a memorial of the founders, written by the **Druids**. Had it been preserved until now, it would have been an invaluable curiosity. 

To offer the reader some compensation for such a loss, I have provided a specimen of supposed **Druid** writing, taken from **Lambecius’s** account of the **Emperor’s** library at **Vienna**. It is written on a very thin plate of gold with a sharp-pointed instrument. It was discovered in an urn at **Vienna**, rolled up in several cases made of other metals, alongside **funeral remains** <note>original Latin: *exuviæ*; refers to items left with the dead</note>. The curious-minded believe it to be one of those epistles which the **Celtic** people were known to send to their friends in the other world. So certain a hope of a future state had the **Druids** instilled in them. The reader may amuse himself by trying to explain it. Writing upon plates of gold or tin is exceedingly ancient, as we see in the Bible in **Job 19:24**.

->[An illustration of a rectangular gold plate containing six lines of mysterious, archaic characters. The symbols resemble a mix of early Greek, reversed Latin letters, and runic shapes, separated by small dots.]<-

**Plutarch**, in his essay *On the Sign of Socrates* <note>original Latin title: *de dæmonio Socratis*</note>, tells a similar story: “About the time of **Agesilaus**, they found a bronze tablet in the tomb of **Alcmena** at **Thebes**, written in unknown characters that seemed to be **Egyptian**. **Chonuphis**, the most learned of the **Egyptian** prophets at that time, was consulted; he confirmed it, stating it was written around the time of **Hercules** and **Proteus**, the king of **Egypt**.” **Tzetzes** <note>A 12th-century Byzantine scholar</note> mentions **Proteus** as a king in Lower **Egypt** by the seaside; he claims he was the son of **Neptune** and **Phoenicia**, thereby dating him back to very ancient times—those of the first famous navigators, our **Hercules** and the **Phoenicians**. He is said to have lived on the island later called **Pharos**, named after the watchtower (lighthouse) erected there. Here **Homer** sings that **Proteus** amuses himself with his **seals** <note>original Latin: *phocæ*; literally "sea-calves"</note>, which undoubtedly refers to his ships. But at

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>STONEHENGE</header>

At that time, everything new and strange was told by the *Greeks* in a mythological way.

<margin>Plate XXXI.</margin> In the year 1635, as they were plowing by the burial mounds <note>referred to as barrows</note> near *Normanton* ditch, they found a large quantity of excellent pewter; they sold so much of it at a low price that they earned 5 pounds, says *Mr. Aubrey* <note>John Aubrey, a 17th-century antiquary</note> in his manuscript collections regarding antiquities of this kind. There are several of these ditches, being very narrow, which run across the downs. I believe they serve as boundaries for "hundreds" <note>old administrative districts</note>, parishes, and so on, such as the reader may observe in my *Plate* XXXI of the barrows in *Lake-field*. I suspect this pewter was actually a tablet with an inscription on it, but falling into the hands of the local countrymen, they could no more recognize the writing than they could interpret it. No doubt this was some of the old *British tin* <note>original Latin: *stannum*</note>, which the *Tyrian Hercules*, surnamed *Melcarthus* <note>a Phoenician god often identified with the Greek Hercules</note>, first brought *from the Tin Island* <note>original Latin: *ex Cassiteride insula*</note>, or *Britain*. This *Hercules* lived in *Abraham*’s time, or soon after.

*Mr. Webb* tells us that the Duke of *Buckingham* dug around *Stonehenge*; I fear this was much to the detriment of the monument. Webb himself did the same and found what he imagined was the cover of an *incense burner* <note>original Latin: *thuribulum*</note>. He would have done well to provide us with a drawing of it. But whatever it was, vessels for incense, oil, flour, salt, wine, and holy water were used by all nations in their religious ceremonies.

*Mr. Thomas Hayward*, the late owner of *Stonehenge*, also dug around it, as he informed Lord *Winchelsea* and myself. He found the heads of oxen and other animal bones, and nothing else. In 1724, when I was there, *Richard Hayns*—an old man from *Amesbury* whom I employed to dig for me in the barrows—found some small, worn-out *Roman* coins at *Stonehenge* among the earth kicked up by the rabbits. He sold one of them for half a crown to *Mr. Merrill* of *Golden Square*, who arrived while I was at the site. The year before, *Hayns* was one of the workmen employed by Lord *Carlton* to dig clay on *Harradon Hill*, east of *Amesbury*, where they found many *Roman* coins, which I saw. I suspect he only pretended to find those at *Stonehenge* for the sake of a reward. My late friend *Dr. Harwood* of *Doctors’ Commons* told me he was once at *Stonehenge* with similar *Roman* coins in his pockets, and one of his companions tried to persuade him to throw some into the rabbit holes; however, the *Doctor* was too honest for that. Nevertheless, even if many such coins were found in *Stonehenge*, they would prove nothing more than that the structure existed when the *Romans* were here, which we already know for certain. I have a brass coin given to me by *John Collins, Esq.*, the excise collector at *Stamford*. The heads of *Julius Caesar* and *Augustus* are on the front <note>original: "averse," meaning the obverse side</note>; the reverse shows a crocodile, a palm branch, and a garland, with the letters COL. NEM. <note>short for *Colonia Nemausus*, the Roman colony at Nîmes in France</note>. It was found on *Salisbury* Plain and might have been left there before the *Roman* conquest of *Britain* under *Claudius* by travelers from *France*, or in later ages; it matters little which.

On *July* 5, 1723, by Lord *Pembroke*’s direction, I dug on the inside of the altar near the middle: 4 feet along the edge of the stone, and 6 feet forward toward the middle of the *inner sanctuary* <note>original Latin: *adytum*</note>. At a foot deep, we reached the solid chalk mixed with flints, which had never been disturbed. The altar was exactly one cubit thick (20 and 4/5 inches) but was broken into two or three pieces by the heavy weight of the *top stone* <note>original: "impost"</note> and one upright stone of that *three-stone structure* <note>original: *trilithon*</note> which stood at the head of the *inner sanctuary* and had fallen upon it. This demonstrates how well-suited the foundation was for this massive work. They dug holes into the solid chalk, which would hold the stones as firmly as if a wall were built around them. No doubt they packed the gaps with flints. However, I had too much respect for the monument to dig anywhere near the standing stones. I found an ox’s tooth above ground, outside the *inner sanctuary* on the right hand of the lowest *three-stone structure* to the north. And this is the complete account of what has been found by digging at *Stonehenge* that I am able to provide.

# STONEHENGE

...at that time of day, everything new and strange was told by the *Greeks* in a mythological way.

<margin>PLATE XXXI.</margin>
In the year 1635, as they were plowing by the burial mounds (barrows) near the *Normanton* ditch, they found a large quantity of excellent pewter—so much that they sold it at a low price for 5 pounds, according to Mr. *Aubrey* <note>John Aubrey (1626–1697), a pioneering English antiquarian</note> in his manuscript collections relating to antiquities of this kind. There are several of these ditches, which are very narrow, running across the downs. I take them to be boundaries of hundreds <note>a historical administrative division of a county</note>, parishes, and so on, such as the reader may observe in my Plate XXXI of the barrows in *Lake-field*. I suspect that the pewter object was actually a tablet with an inscription on it, but falling into the hands of the local countrymen, they could no more see the writing than they could interpret it. No doubt this was some of the ancient *British tin* <note>original: "stannum"</note>, which the *Tyrian Hercules*, known as *Melqart* <note>original: "Melcarthus"</note>, first brought *from the Tin Island* <note>original: "ex Cassiteride insula"</note>, or *Britain*. This *Hercules* lived in *Abraham*'s time, or soon after.

Mr. *Webb* <note>John Webb (1611–1672), an architect who published a book on Stonehenge based on the notes of Inigo Jones</note> tells us that the Duke of *Buckingham* dug around *Stonehenge*; I fear much to the detriment of the monument. Webb himself did the same and found what he imagined was the cover of a <term>thuribulum</term> <note>a vessel or censer used for burning incense</note>. He would have done well to have provided a drawing of it. But whatever it was, vessels for incense, oil, flour, salt, wine, and holy water were used by all nations in their religious ceremonies.

Mr. *Thomas Hayward*, the late owner of *Stonehenge*, dug around it, as he informed Lord *Winchelsea* and myself. He found the heads of oxen and other animal bones, and nothing else. In 1724, when I was there, *Richard Hayns*, an old man of *Amesbury* <note>original: "Ambresbury"</note> whom I employed to dig for me in the barrows, found some small, worn-out *Roman* coins at *Stonehenge* among the earth rooted up by rabbits. He sold one of them for half a crown to Mr. *Merrill* of *Golden Square*, who arrived there while I was at the site. The year before, *Hayns* was one of the workmen employed by Lord *Carleton* to dig clay on *Harradon* hill, east of *Amesbury*, where they found many *Roman* coins which I saw myself. I suspect he only pretended to find those at *Stonehenge* for the sake of the reward. My friend, the late Dr. *Harwood* of *Doctors-Commons*, told me he was once at *Stonehenge* with such *Roman* coins in his pockets, and that one of his companions tried to persuade him to throw some of them into the rabbit holes; but the Doctor was more honest. Nevertheless, even if many such coins were found in *Stonehenge*, they would prove nothing more than that the work was already standing when the *Romans* were here, which we are already certain of. I have a brass coin given to me by *John Collins*, Esq., the collector of the excise at *Stamford*. It shows the heads of *Julius* and *Augustus* back-to-back <note>original: "averse"</note>; on the reverse is a crocodile, a palm branch, and a garland, with the inscription *COL. NEM.* <note>abbreviation for Colonia Nemausus, the Roman colony at modern-day Nîmes, France</note>. It was found on *Salisbury* plain and might have been left there before the *Roman* conquest of *Britain* under *Claudius* by people coming here from *France*, or in later ages; it matters not which.

*July* 5, 1723. By Lord *Pembroke*'s direction, I dug on the inside of the altar near the middle: 4 feet along the edge of the stone, and 6 feet forward toward the middle of the *<term>adytum</term>* <note>the inner sanctum or most sacred part of a temple</note>. At a foot deep, we reached the solid chalk mixed with flints, which had never been disturbed. The altar was exactly a cubit thick (20 and 4/5 inches), but broken into two or three pieces by the ponderous mass of the lintel <note>original: "impost"</note> and one upright stone of that *<term>trilithon</term>* <note>a structure consisting of two large vertical stones supporting a third stone set horizontally across the top</note> which stood at the upper end of the *inner sanctum*, having fallen upon it. From this, the suitability of the foundation for this huge work is apparent. They dug holes in the solid chalk, which would of itself hold up the stones as firmly as if a wall were built around them. No doubt they packed the gaps with flints. But I had too much respect for the work to dig anywhere near the standing stones. I picked up an ox's tooth from the surface, outside the *inner sanctum* on the right hand of the lowermost *trilithon*, to the north. And this is the full account of what I can report being found by digging at *Stonehenge*.

# CHAP. VII.

### Concerning the area surrounding Stonehenge, the bowing stones, and the method of sacrifice.

<margin>Plate XXIII</margin>
Something has already been said regarding the court surrounding the temple of *Stonehenge*, the two stones standing within the *rampart* <term>vallum: an earthen wall or bank used for enclosure</term>, and the two notable cavities that correspond with them. I suspected these cavities were places where two large basins of water once stood, used for the temple service when religious rites were performed here. I attempted to illustrate this by comparing it to a coin from the city of *Heliopolis* <note>The ancient Egyptian "City of the Sun." The author often compares British Druids to other ancient solar-worshipping civilizations.</note>. The diameter of *Stonehenge* itself is 60 cubits <term>cubit: an ancient unit of measurement; the author believes the Druids used a specific "Druid cubit" of about 20.8 inches</term>, and another 60 cubits reaches the inner edge of the circular ditch surrounding the court. This ditch was originally nearly 30 cubits wide, but through the long passage of time—and the endless number of coaches and horses arriving daily to see the site—it has been significantly leveled. The entire diameter of the court, reaching to the outer edge of the ditch, is four times 60 cubits, which is about 410 feet. The five outer circles of the ditch are laid out with radii of 80, 90, 100, 110, and 120 cubits.

Right upon the inner edge of the ditch, at the entrance from the avenue, lies a very large stone, which is currently flat on the ground <note>This is likely the stone now known as the "Slaughter Stone."</note>. Mr. *Webb* <note>John Webb (1611–1672), an architect who published the theories of Inigo Jones, arguing that Stonehenge was a Roman temple</note>, on page 57, claims to give us its measurements, but he confuses it with the other two stones mentioned previously within the *rampart*. In reality, this stone has no relation or similarity in proportion to those. He does this to support his idea that there were three entrances to the *area*, based on his theory of equilateral triangles. He states at the letter F that "the parallel stones on the inside of the trench were four feet wide and three feet thick; but they lie so broken and ruined by time that their height cannot be determined, let alone measured exactly." He says this, but it is *against all logic* <note>original: "invita Minervâ," a Latin idiom meaning "without natural inspiration" or "against the grain"</note>; for all three stones, to all appearances, are as little changed from their original size as any other stones in the work. The two stones within the *rampart* are very small, and they always were. One stands upright; the other leans slightly, likely because of idle people digging around it. This stone at the entrance, however, is massive—nearly as large as any in the entire monument—and seems just as little altered from its original form, though it was perhaps toppled by the same foolish curiosity of diggers. Instead of *Webb’s* four-foot width, it is nearly seven feet wide; or, to speak in Druid measurements, four cubits. It is currently over 20 feet long. If it originally stood upright with a slight lean, it was one of those stones the *Welsh* call *bowing-stones* <note>original: "crwm lechen"</note>. Regardless, Mr. *Webb* must be distorting the truth significantly by claiming this stone and the two previous ones are at all alike in size, location, or use.

But he misleads even more regarding the next stone, which is undoubtedly a *bowing-stone* <note>original: "crwm leche"</note> still standing in its original position and place within the avenue <note>This refers to the "Heel Stone."</note>. It is of similar dimensions to the other, though not as well-shaped, and stands in the same manner on the left hand (the south) of the avenue’s center line. I suspect the Druids took care to make the other stone slightly more shapely because it was inside the sacred area and closer to the holy structure. There is a distance of 119 feet between them—or, more accurately, 80 cubits. Mr. *Webb* shrinks this interval to about 43 feet and imagines there was another stone to match it on the right-hand side, as well as another to match the one inside the ditch. He imagines the same for those previously mentioned, both inside and outside the ditch, at his two imaginary entrances. But of these, there is *no sign or trace* <note>original: "nec vola nec vestigium"</note>, and I dare say there never was. This stone has a hole in it, which is often observed in similar stones placed near temples like this, as we shall see as this work progresses. The stone is 24 feet in circumference and 16 

<sig>K</sig>
high <note>catchword: high</note>

<summary>This page describes the outer boundaries and measurements of the Stonehenge courtyard, specifically critiquing John Webb's earlier Roman-centric theories. The author identifies the large stones at the entrance as "bowing stones" used in Druid rituals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 33 regarding the outer layout of Stonehenge, specifically detailing the entrance stones and the author's theories on the rituals once performed there.</meta>

# STONEHENGE

<note>The text here continues a description of a specific stone near the entrance.</note> high above ground, 9 <term>cubit: an ancient unit of measurement, roughly 20 inches</term> broad, 6 thick. The use of it I cannot certainly tell; but I am inclined to think that as part of the religious worship in old patriarchal times <note>The author believes the Druids practiced an "original" religion similar to that of the biblical patriarchs like Abraham.</note> consisted in a solemn adoration, or three silent bowings: the first bowing might be performed at this stone, just outside the ditch, the second perhaps at the next stone, just within the ditch. Then they turned by that stone to the left hand, as the custom was, in a procession round the temple, both the priests and animals for sacrifice. At those two stones and water-vases, probably there were some washings, <term>lustration: a ritual purification or cleansing involving water</term>, or sprinklings with holy water, and other ceremonies, which I do not pretend to determine for certain. Then upon the entry into the temple, perhaps they made the third bow, as in the presence of the Deity. After this, in the *court*, we may suppose the priests prepared the <term>hecatomb: a great public sacrifice, traditionally of one hundred oxen</term> and customary sacrifices. If that great stone just within the ditch always lay, as it does now, flat on the ground and *in situ* <note>original: "in situ" — in its original place</note> (which I am unwilling to believe), then, I apprehend, it was a table for dressing the victims. *Ezekiel*, in describing the temple of *Jerusalem*, speaks of such in the entry, chapter 40, verses 39, 40, 41, 42, 43.

It is just to think that the ancient form of sacrificing here, like that of the *Romans*, *Greeks*, or older nations, was pretty much the same as that among the *Jews*, and *that* as in patriarchal times; and in short, no other than the original practice of mankind since the first institution of sacrifices at the fall of man. Therefore we shall subjoin it from *Homer’s* description in *Iliad* Book I. It aligns extremely well, in all appearance, with the place and temple before us.

->Straightway in haste, a chosen hecatomb<-
->To God, prepared, the well-built altar round,<-
->They place in order. Then their hands they wash,<-
->And take the salted meal. Aloud the priest,<-
->With hands uplifted, for the assembly prays.<-
->After the prayers, they waved the salted meal,<-
->And then retiring flay the animals.<-
->The skins being stripped, they cut off both the thighs,<-
->And cover them with <term>cawl: a fatty membrane or veil</term>; first offered crude.<-
->The priest then burns a part on plates,† thereon red wine,<-
->Libation poured. The ministering young men<-
->Stand by him, with their five-fold spits in hand.<-
->But when the thighs are burnt, out of the rest<-
->Entrails and flesh, <term>haslets: organ meats, often used in stews or roasts</term> and steaks they make,<-
->Upon the spits transfixed. Then roasted well<-
->They set all forth. After the duty done;<-
->A feast they next prepare. Plenty of food<-
->Distributed around, cheerful repast.<-
->Banquet being over, the youths huge goblets crown,<-
->And fill to all in cups. Then sacred hymns<-
->Sung to the Deity, conclude the day.<-

† In another place he adds,

->With choice cloven bits of wood,<-
->Without leaves ———<-

These are most ancient rites, symbolical of the purity of the sacrifice of the *Messiah*, pointed at by, and derived from the *Mosaic dispensation* <note>The religious system and laws established by Moses in the Old Testament.</note>, where everything of sacred purpose was to be perfect.

Thus much is sufficient to give the reader an idea of the ancient manner of sacrificing, such, no doubt, as was practiced at this very place—entirely following the *Hebrew* rite. I suppose only the priests and chief personages came within the *area*, who made the procession with the sacrifices along the avenue. The multitude kept outside, on foot or in their chariots.

<meta>catchword: CHAP.</meta>

<summary>The author describes a hypothetical Druidic ritual at Stonehenge involving three ceremonial bows and animal sacrifice, drawing parallels between these rites and those described by Homer and the Hebrew Bible. He suggests the "Slaughter Stone" served as a table for preparing victims and concludes that Druidic practices were a form of ancient "patriarchal" religion.</summary>

### STONEHENGE

high above ground, 9 broad, 6 thick. The use of it I can’t certainly tell; but I am inclined to think, that as part of the religious worship in old patriarchal times <note>The author refers to the era of the biblical "Patriarchs" like Abraham, as he believed the Druids practiced a form of religion inherited from them.</note>, consisted in a solemn adoration, or three silent bowings: the first bowing might be performed at this stone, just without the ditch, the second perhaps at the next stone, just within the ditch. Then they turned by that stone to the left hand, as the manner was, in a procession round the temple, both the priests and animals for sacrifice. At those two stones and water-vases, probably there were some washings, <term>lustrations</term> <note>Ritual purification or cleansing ceremonies using water.</note>, or sprinklings with holy water, and other ceremonies, which I don’t pretend to ascertain. Then upon the entry into the temple, perhaps they made the third bow, as in presence of the Deity. After this, in the court, we may suppose the priests prepared the <term>hecatombs</term> <note>A massive public sacrifice, traditionally involving 100 oxen, though used here to mean any large-scale ritual offering.</note> and customary sacrifices. If that great stone just within the ditch always lay, as it does now, flat on the ground, and **in its original place** <note>original: "in situ"</note>, (which I am not unwilling to believe,) then, I apprehend, it was a table for dressing the victims. *Ezekiel*, in describing the temple of *Jerusalem*, speaks of such in the entry, chapter 40: 39, 40, 41, 42, 43. <note>The author uses Roman numerals xl (40), but modern readers will recognize the Bible chapter more easily in digits.</note> It is just to think, the ancient form of sacrificing here, like that of the *Romans*, *Greeks* or elder nations, was pretty much the same as that among the *Jews*, and that as in patriarchal times; and in short, no other than the original practice of mankind, since the first institution of sacrifices, at the fall. Therefore we shall subjoin it from *Homer’s* description, in *Iliad* Book 1. It quadrates <note>Fits or matches.</note> extremely well, in all appearance, with the place and temple before us.

> Straightway in haste, a chosen hecatomb
> To God, prepared, the well-built altar round,
> They place in order. Then their hands they wash,
> And take the salted meal. Aloud the priest,
> With hands uplifted, for the assembly prays.
> After the prayers, they waved the salted meal,
> And then retiring slay the animals.
> The skins being stripped, they cut off both the thighs,
> And cover them with <term>caul</term> <note>A fatty membrane found around the organs of cattle or sheep.</note>; first offered crude.
> The priest then burns a part on plates,† thereon red wine,
> Libation poured. The ministering young men
> Stand by him, with their five-fold spits in hand.
> But when the thighs are burnt, out of the rest
> Entrails and flesh, <term>haslets</term> <note>Edible internal organs, such as heart or liver.</note> and pieces they make,
> Upon the spits transfixed. Then roasted well
> They set all forth. After the duty done;
> A feast they next prepare. Plenty of food
> Distributed around, cheerful repast.
> Banquet being over, the youths huge goblets crown,
> And fill to all in cups. Then sacred hymns
> Sung to the Deity, conclude the day.

† In another place he adds,

Without leaves----------
These are most ancient rites, symbolical of the purity of the sacrifice of the *Messiah*, pointed at by, and derived from the *Mosaic* dispensation <note>The religious system and laws established through Moses in the Old Testament.</note>, where every thing of sacred purpose was to be perfect.

Thus much is sufficient to give the reader an idea of the ancient manner of sacrificing, such, no doubt as was practiced at this very place entirely the *Hebrew* rite. I suppose only the priests and chief personages came within the area, who made the procession with the sacrifices along the avenue. The multitude kept without, on foot or in their chariots.

DESCRIBED.

## CHAPTER VIII.

### *Concerning the Avenue to Stonehenge.*

The Avenue leading to *Stonehenge* was never noticed by any who have previously written about it, even though it is a very elegant and quite visible feature. It aligns, as we have mentioned before, with the principal axis of the entire work—the northeast—roughly where the sun rises during the longest days of the year. *Plutarch*, in his biography of the Roman king *Numa*, says that the ancients observed a rule of positioning their temples so the front would face the rising sun. *Promachidas* of *Heracleum* and *Dionysius Thrax* <note>Two ancient Greek scholars and grammarians</note> also noted this same practice. This was done in imitation of the *Mosaic* tabernacle <note>The portable sanctuary described in the Hebrew Bible</note> and *Solomon’s* temple; it was likely an ancient patriarchal rite. This avenue extends for more than 1,700 feet in a straight line, descending gracefully toward the bottom of the valley. I have observed that the earth from the flanking ditches was thrown inward, and it appears that some turf from both sides was piled onto the avenue to raise it slightly above the level of the downs. The two ditches remain perfectly parallel all the way to the bottom, separated by 40 <term>cubits</term> <note>An ancient unit of measurement; the author used a "Druid cubit" of approximately 20.8 inches</note>. About halfway down, there is a natural dip in the land which provides a pleasing variety to the landscape. 

As I have said, *Stonehenge* is not located on the highest part of the hill. I discovered the reason why the Druids placed it exactly where it is: it is precisely 1,000 cubits from the bottom of the valley to the entrance of the monument area. When I first began my investigation into this noble work, I thought the avenue ended here, and Mr. *Roger Gale* <note>An English antiquary and friend of the author</note> and I measured it with a chain to that point. However, in a later year, I found it extended much farther. At the bottom of the valley, it divides into two branches. The eastern branch continues a long way from here, heading directly east toward an ancient crossing point on the river *Avon* called *Radfin*, and beyond that, the vista <note>original: "visto"</note> of the avenue points directly toward *Harradon* Hill on the other side of the river.

<margin>PLATE XXVIII</margin>
The western branch, starting from this same point at the bottom of the hill (1,000 cubits from the work at *Stonehenge*, as mentioned), begins with a similar curve. However, it does not immediately straighten out like the first branch; instead, it continues to curve along the foot of the hill until it meets what I call the <term>cursus</term> <note>Latin for "track" or "course"; a large, elongated Neolithic earthwork</note>. This is another newly discovered curiosity belonging to this site, and it greatly expands the concept we should have of the magnificence and enormous scale of the place. The temple we have been describing so far—as significant as it truly is on its own—now appears to be only a small part of the whole. I shall therefore describe all these parts separately to make them easier to understand, and then show their connection and the relationship they have to one another as best as I can. 

It is not easy to grasp all at once the immense scale of thought possessed by the people who founded this monument; they incorporated all the adjacent countryside and the natural landscape hereabouts to contribute its part to the work. Therefore, I will discuss it back and forth—first traveling from *Stonehenge* to its furthest end (or more accurately, its beginning) and then returning again. I will explain its current condition and what is reasonable to assume was its original state when the Druids first made their design. This, together with the various views I have drawn, will give us as good an understanding of the whole as we can expect today. Perhaps it will also preserve the memory of it for the future, when the traces of this mighty work are erased by the plow—a fate I fear is inevitable. That tool is encroaching too much upon the ancient and peaceful pastoral life here and everywhere else in *England*, and through the destructive practice of enclosing land, it impoverishes and depopulates the countryside.

<margin>PLATE XXVIII</margin>
At the bottom of the valley, and at the end of the straight section of the *Stonehenge* avenue—1,000 cubits from *Stonehenge*, as we said—the eastern wing of the avenue...

<summary>This page describes the Avenue leading to Stonehenge, noting its alignment with the summer solstice sunrise and its division into two branches. The author introduces the "cursus" and laments the destruction of ancient landscapes by modern agricultural plowing.</summary>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed topographical survey of the Stonehenge landscape, specifically focusing on 'The Avenue'—the ancient processional way leading to the monument—and its relationship to nearby burial mounds.</meta>

# STONEHENGE

<margin>Plate 26</margin>
The avenue turns off to the right, with a circular sweep, and then in a straight line proceeds eastward up the hill. It goes just between those two most conspicuous groups of barrows <note>Barrows are ancient burial mounds or tumuli</note>, crowning the ridge of that hill eastward of <term>Stonehenge</term>; between it and <term>Vespasian's camp</term> <note>An Iron Age hillfort near Amesbury, which early antiquarians mistakenly associated with the Roman General Vespasian</note>, separated from them both by a deep valley on each side. These two groups of barrows are generally called the <term>seven kings' graves</term> each. I call that most northerly group the "old seven kings' graves," for there are really seven, though only six are most apparent; they are all set at a greater distance from each other, all broader, flatter, and as it is most reasonable to suppose, older than the other group. The other group appears closer together and much later than the former. Therefore I call these, being southward and directly between <term>Stonehenge</term> and the town of <term>Ambresbury</term> <note>Modern-day Amesbury</note>, the "new seven kings' barrows." Of the seven old ones, the most northerly one, and probably the oldest, is exceedingly flat and as it were, almost sunk into the earth with age; so that it is scarce visible at a distance. The avenue runs up to the top of the hill, just between them: and they make, as it were, wings to it, and I believe were designed as such when set there. When the avenue first turns off in the valley, it is much obscured by the wheels of carriages going over it for a great distance; for this is the road to <term>Lavington</term>. Nevertheless, a curious eye, without difficulty, sees all the traces of it sufficiently until it has moved higher up the easy ascent of the hill and out of the common road. Then it is very apparent and consists of the two little ditches as before (when coming directly from <term>Stonehenge</term>), exactly parallel, and still 40 cubits apart <note>The author, William Stukeley, believed the builders used a specific "Druid cubit" of about 20.8 inches</note>. And it is made with the same degree of variation, or about 6 degrees southward from the true east point. So that it is evident again that the <term>Druids</term> intended it should go full east, but their compass by which they set it varied so much at that time, according to my opinion of the matter. To perpetuate the mark of it as much as I can: I measured the distance from its southern ditch to the ditch of the nearest (that is, the most northerly) of the new seven kings' barrows; when in the right line of those seven barrows, it is 257 feet. I know not whether there was any design in it, but it is exactly 150 cubits. From the northern ditch of the avenue here to the nearest of the old seven kings' barrows is 350 feet; which is exactly 200 cubits.

<margin>Plate 24</margin>
While we are here upon the elevation of this hill, between these two groups of barrows, it is 2700 feet from the beginning of this wing of the avenue at the bottom of the valley where it commences. It still continues in the very same direction eastward, until unfortunately broken off by the plowed ground, 300 feet from hence. This plowed ground continues for a mile together, as far as the river's side at <term>Ambresbury</term>. So that it is impossible to trace it any farther. The first plowed field, that to the southward, is Mr. *Hayward's*; the other belongs to a different estate, called <term>Countess-farm</term> <note>A farm near Amesbury that still exists today, named for the Countess of Salisbury</note>.

<margin>Plate 27</margin>
And the plowing of these two goes on at right angles to one another. That piece on the north side of the avenue, of the latter tenure, goes along the line of the avenue; it is long and narrow, and has (as is usual with greedy farmers) encroached upon and swallowed up so much of the length of the avenue. And that amounts to 750 feet more in length, which must certainly be added to the avenue. This is all along the eastern declivity of the hill we are upon—that of the twice seven kings' graves—and reaches near the bottom of the valley, between it and the hill whereon stands <term>Vespasian's camp</term>. Now reason and the judgment I have gained in conversing with works of this kind tell me the founders would never begin this avenue at the bottom of a valley, but rather on a conspicuous height, which is visible from a great distance of country round. We must suppose the intent of the avenue was to direct the <term>religious procession</term> to the <term>temple</term>; and that at the beginning of it, they made fires early in the morning of that day when they held their grand <term>festivals</term>, to give notice to all the adjacent country. Therefore when we cross this valley still eastward, with the...

<meta>This page continues the physical description of the landscape surrounding Stonehenge, specifically focusing on the "Avenue" or ceremonial approach, and begins a comparative study of round temples in antiquity.</meta>

# DESCRIBED.

Next you descend into the valley to the union of the wings of the avenue, and ascend the agreeable part of it, to the temple. Along here went the sacred pomp <note>By "pomp," the author means a formal ceremonial procession.</note>. How would it delight one to have seen it in its first splendor!

->---*Even now it is a joy to lead the solemn processions to the shrines, and to see the slaughtered bullocks.* <note>original: "Jam nunc solennes ducere pompas Ad delubra juvat, cæsosque videre juvenos." (Vergil, Georgics III, 487-488). The original Latin refers to bullocks or steers (juvencos) sacrificed in ritual.</note>---<-

<margin>TABLE I.</margin>
I have often admired the delicacy of this ascent to the temple. As soon as you mount from the bottom, it is level for a great way together: and the whole length of it is a kind of ridge, for it slopes off both ways from on each side; so that the rain runs off every way. Just about half way there is a depressure, as a pause or foot pace, showing one half of the avenue ascending, the other descending, both magnificent, in the ancient <term>gusto</term> <note>From the Italian for "taste" or "style."</note>. There was a temple of <term>Jupiter Labradaeus</term> <note>A localized version of the Greek god Zeus/Jupiter worshipped in Caria, often depicted carrying a double-headed axe.</note> near <term>Mylasa</term> <note>An ancient city in southwestern Turkey.</note> a city of <term>Caria</term>, much frequented. The way leading thither was called sacred, and paved 60 furlongs, through which their procession went. <term>Philostratus</term> <note>A Greek scholar of the Roman Imperial period.</note> says, you went to the temple of <term>Diana</term> at <term>Ephesus</term>, by a stone portico of a <term>stadium</term> <note>A Greek unit of distance, roughly 600 feet.</note>. <term>Pausanias</term> <note>A famous Greek traveler and geographer.</note> in his description of <term>Phocis</term> says, the avenue to the temple of <term>Minerva Cranea</term> near <term>Elatea</term> is ascending, but so gently that it is imperceptible. Again in Chapter X. we read of a paved way, to the <term>oracle</term> at <term>Delphi</term>. But the natural pavement of our avenue is much finer. I take notice, that <term>Jupiter Labradaeus</term> was a statue holding a <term>halberd</term> <note>A two-handed pole weapon with an axe blade and a spike.</note> in his hand, which instrument like a <term>securis</term> or <term>Amazon axe</term> <note>A double-headed battle-axe (labrys).</note>, was as a <term>scepter</term> to the <term>Lydian kings</term>. And apparently our <term>English halberd</term> is the very word, with an aspirated way of pronunciation prefixed, <term>Labrada</term>. So our <term>Druids</term> carried about a sharp brass instrument which we often find, called a <term>celt</term>, (I know not whence,) with which they used to cut the <term>mistletoe</term>, at their great festival in <term>midwinter</term>. I have represented one hanging at our <term>Druid's girdle</term>, in TABLE I.; it was to be put into the slit at the end of his staff, when used. But of this hereafter. Now with the <term>Poet</term> in his celebrated <term>Ode</term>

And now we are returned to the sacred fabric, we will discourse a little upon these <term>temples</term> in general, and so conclude this chapter.
In <term>Macrobius's</term> <term>Saturnalia</term> <note>A 5th-century Roman work containing discussions on history, myth, and religion.</note> Book I, chapter 18, mention is made of a famous round <term>temple</term> in <term>Thrace</term>, where they celebrate most magnificent religious rites. It is upon the hill <term>Zilmissus</term>. The <term>temple</term> is open at top. I suppose like ours, not a little round hole like as in the <term>Pantheon</term> <note>The famous Roman temple with a central opening (oculus) in its dome.</note>, nor is it a small round <term>sacellum</term> <note>A small, roofless shrine.</note> like those little round <term>temples</term> at <term>Rome</term> to <term>Romulus</term>, to <term>Vesta</term>, etc. It is not reasonable to think they should build a <term>Pantheon</term> in <term>Thrace</term>, nor can I understand it otherwise, than that it was like our <term>Stonehenge</term>, and in truth an ancient patriarchal structure of a primitive model. The <term>Deity</term> here worshipped was called <term>Sabazius</term> says he; some make him <term>Jupiter</term>, some the <term>sun</term>, some <term>Bacchus</term>. These are the first perversions of the <term>Jehovah</term> of the <term>Jews</term>. In my judgment, the name <term>Sabazius</term> is a corruption of the <term>Hebrew</term> name of <term>God</term> <term>Sabaoth</term> <note>original Hebrew: צבאות</note>, <term>God of Hosts</term> <note>original Latin: "Deus exercituum"</note>, a title that would well suit the warlike <term>Thracians</term>. In time <term>Idolatry</term> debased every thing. ## DESCRIBED.

Next you descend into the valley to the union of the wings of the avenue, and ascend the agreeable part of it, to the temple. Along here went the sacred pomp <note>A formal procession or parade.</note>. How would it delight one to have seen it in its first splendor!

> *Even now it is a joy to lead the solemn processions*
> *To the shrines, and to see the slaughtered bullocks.*
<note>original Latin: "Jam nunc solennes ducere pompas / Ad delubra juvat, cæsosque videre juvencos." from Virgil's Georgics.</note>
Virgil.

I have often admired the delicacy of this ascent to the temple. As soon as you mount from the bottom, it is level for a great way together: and the whole length of it is a kind of ridge, for it slopes off both ways from on each side; so that the rain runs off every way. Just about half way there is a depression, as a pause or foot pace, showing one half of the avenue ascending, the other descending, both magnificent, in the ancient <unclear>ancient</unclear> style <note>original: "gusto"</note>. There was a temple of <term>Jupiter Labradæus</term> <note>A local version of Zeus from Caria, often depicted carrying a double-headed axe.</note> near <term>Mylasa</term> <note>An ancient city in what is now Turkey.</note> a city of <term>Caria</term>, much frequented. The Way leading thither was called sacred, and paved 60 furlongs, through which their procession went. <term>Philostratus</term> says, you went to the temple of <term>Diana</term> at <term>Ephesus</term>, by a stone portico of a stadium. <term>Pausanias</term> in his work *Description of Greece* <note>original: "Phocicis"</note> says, the avenue to the temple of <term>Minerva</term> at <term>Cranea</term> near <term>Elatea</term> is ascending, but so gently that it is imperceptible. Again in Chapter 10, we read of a paved way, to the oracle at <term>Delphos</term>. But the natural pavement of our avenue is much finer. I take notice, that <term>Jupiter Labradæus</term> was a statue holding a <term>halbard</term> in his hand, which instrument like a <term>securis</term> <note>A Latin term for a battle-axe.</note> or Amazonian axe, was as a <term>scepter</term> to the <term>Lydian</term> kings. And apparently our <term>English</term> <term>halbard</term> is the very word, with an aspirated way of pronunciation prefixed, <term>Labrada</term>. So our <term>Druids</term> carried an instrument which we often find, called a <term>celt</term> <note>A prehistoric stone or bronze tool, often an axe head.</note>, (I know not whence,) with which they used to cut the <term>Mistletoe</term>, at their great festival in midwinter. I have represented one hanging at our <term>Druid's</term> girdle, in <margin>Plate 1.</margin> it was to be put into the slit at the end of his staff, when used. But of this hereafter. Now with the <term>Poet</term> in his celebrated <term>Ode</term>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the patriarchal and biblical origins of Stonehenge, specifically focusing on the divine names used by ancient peoples and the structural parallels between Druidical circles and early Middle Eastern places of worship.</meta>

# STONEHENGE

frequently pronouncing **Jehovah** <note>Hebrew: יהוה</note>, and this sacred cry is truly no other than what frequently occurs in holy scripture: **Jehovah of Hosts** <note>Hebrew: יהוה צבאות; original: *Jehovah Sabaoth*</note>. He is the king of glory, *Psalm* 24:10. But I have discussed this topic in my *Ancient Sacred Writings* <note>original: *Paleographia Sacra*</note> No. I, which will be continued.

*Diodorus Siculus* <note>A Greek historian from the 1st century BCE</note> in his second book mentions a very eminent temple of a round form among the *Hyperboreans* <term>Hyperboreans: In Greek mythology, a race of giants who lived "beyond the North Wind" in a land of perpetual sunshine</term>, as he calls them, who inhabit an island situated in the ocean opposite *Gaul* <note>Modern-day France</note>, which is not smaller than *Sicily*. He gives an odd account from there mixed with fable, and seemingly some reports of *Stonehenge* itself.

Mr. *Toland* <note>John Toland (1670–1722), an Irish scholar who wrote about the Druids</note> is confident this Hyperborean region is our *Shetland* isles, from where came *Abaris* the Druid and Hyperborean philosopher, famous in *Grecian* history. While I am writing this, *March* 6, 1739-40 <note>The double year reflects the transition from the Julian to the Gregorian calendar</note>, we had an account read before the *Royal Society* <note>The UK’s national academy of sciences</note> much confirming Mr. *Toland*’s notion; speaking of the admirable temperature of the air there, not subject to such extremes or such sudden changes as even in *Britain* itself. There are such temples as ours there.

*Arnobius* <note>An Early Christian apologist</note> in Book VI, speaking of the origin of temples, says: “We don't make temples to the Gods as if we designed to shelter them from the rain, the wind, or the sun; but so that we may present ourselves before them within those walls, and by our prayers, in a way, speak to them as if they were present.” We may well affirm this of our temple, built after the manner of the patriarchal ones, though probably an improvement and somewhat more magnificent. Ours consists of two ovals and two circles. Many in our island, which I suppose are older than *Stonehenge*, consist of one oval—or a niche-like figure made of only three stones (of which our <term>adytum: the innermost, most sacred part of a temple</term> <unclear>adytum</unclear> is a more magnificent specimen)—and a circle of rough stones fixed in the ground; of which our work, crowned with a circular cornice <note>original: "cornish"</note>, is a more magnificent specimen. Sometimes I find a niche without a circle, and sometimes a circle without a niche. We may well say the circle is analogous to our chapels, churches, or cathedrals, according to their different sizes; the niches correspond to our choirs, altars, and the more sacred parts of the building—the more immediate place of the residence of the Deity. They are what the *Turks* and *Arabians* now call the *qibla* <note>original: *kebla*; the direction faced during prayer</note>, derived, as we said before, from patriarchal practice, and particularly from the great patriarch *Abraham*.

I do not doubt that the altars which he and his descendants made, mentioned in scripture, were a stone upon the ground in front of three set in a niche-like figure, with the whole enclosed in a circle of stones. At other times they set only one stone for a *qibla*, as sometimes our ancestors did likewise. This practice was spread generally among all ancient nations. Among many it was forgotten, or not practiced, where they had very little religion at all. Among others, after idolatry had prevailed, they thought all former ways of worship were like their own, and mistook the stones which were *qiblas* or places of worship for the actual objects of worship. Hence *Maximus of Tyre* <note>A Greek philosopher of the 2nd century CE</note> says the *Arabians* worshipped he knew not what, for he saw only a great stone. This was, no doubt, the *qibla* toward which they directed their devotion, as they had learned from *Abraham* or similar patriarchal ancestors. So *Pausanias* in his *Description of Greece: Achaia* <note>original: *Achaicis*</note> says the ancient *Greeks* worshipped unhewn stones instead of statues; more particularly among the *Pharii*, near the statue of *Mercury*, there were 30 square stones which they worshipped. If our author could not make his narration agreeable to common sense, he might well mistake this ancient patriarchal temple, somewhat like our *Stonehenge*, for a circle of deities—he himself being a stranger to any worship other than that of images. I shall handle this matter more largely hereafter, and now let us descend again from the temple to the *cursus* <term>cursus: a large, ancient parallel bank of earth, often thought by early antiquarians to be a Roman racecourse</term>. I would only close this chapter with this short reflection: this avenue is proof enough (if any were needed) that our work is a temple, not a monument <note>In this context, a "monument" refers to a tomb or memorial for the dead</note>, as some writers would have it. But that idea requires no formal refutation.

->CHAP.<-
