->### DESCRIBED.<-

...ward. In order to form our hypothesis, I have set down a scheme of the state of the <term>variation</term> <note>Now called magnetic declination: the angle between true north and magnetic north, which changes over time.</note> in England, from the best observations I could meet with. Dr. <note>Edmond Halley (1656–1742), the English astronomer famous for calculating the orbit of the comet that bears his name.</note> *Halley* takes notice, that the variation at *Paris* is always 2 degrees and a half more easterly than with us. *Orontius Finæus* <note>Oronce Finé (1494–1555), a French mathematician and cartographer.</note> in 1550 observed it to be there, about 9 degrees, easterly; therefore to reduce it <note>To adjust the Paris observation to an English equivalent.</note>, I have stated it at 11 degrees 30 minutes, and from thence continued it, to the present time, as in the ensuing table.

| Year of our Lord | Observation | Variation degrees, minutes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1550 | By *Finæus* | 11 30 east |
| 1580 | Mr. *Burroughs* | 11 15 east |
| 1600 | —————— | 8 0 east |
| 1622 | Mr. *Gunter* | 6 0 east |
| 1634 | Mr. *Gellibrand* | 4 5 east |
| 1642 | —————— | 3 5 east |
| 1657 | Mr. *Bond* | 0 0 |
| 1665 | Mr. *Bond* | 1 22 west |
| 1666 | Capt. *Sturmy* | 1 27 west |
| 1667 | Capt. *Sturmy* | 1 33 west |
| 1672 | Dr. *Halley* | 2 30 west |
| 1683 | —————— | 4 30 west |
| 1685 | —————— | 5 5 west |
| 1692 | —————— | 6 0 west |
| 1723 | —————— | 11 0 west |
| 1733 | —————— | 12 0 west |
| 1740 | —————— | 15 45 west |

By this table it appears, that in the space of 180 years, the variation of the magnetic needle in *England* has shifted from 11 degrees and a half eastward, to 11 degrees and a half westward. In 90 years—the midpoint of those extremes, which was 1657—there was no variation at all; the needle pointing due north and south. But alas, our observations extend no farther. We know not the limit of the variation on either hand, nor the speed of its motion when it reaches those bounds. Mr. *George Graham* thinks it is now near the western limit. It is very slow, in all probability, when it is upon the return and, as it were, stationary; like the sun’s motion at the <term>tropics</term> <note>Referring to the solstices, where the sun appears to "stand still" before reversing direction.</note>, when it is returning. So the precise determination of its cycle and its motion is reserved for remote posterity. Dr. *Halley* conjectures that the whole period of variation is completed in about 700 years. Upon this supposition, in general, we may thus found our conjecture of the time of the building of *Stonehenge*.

By what we can find, the variation is about 9 minutes in a year, or a degree and a half in 10 years, at this part of its cycle. Now I observed at *Stonehenge* that the eastern wing of the avenue, the <term>cursus</term> <note>A large, ancient earthwork enclosure near Stonehenge, named from the Latin word for "racecourse."</note>, and other parts belonging to the temple, abated somewhat in their variation eastward, being somewhat less than that of the temple itself. It is highly reasonable to believe that the great work of *Stonehenge* could not take less than ten years in building, and that those other works were made in succeeding years, not long after it was finished. From hence I gather which way the magnetic variation was moving at the time of founding *Stonehenge*, <note>original: "viz."</note> namely, from east toward no variation and so to west. This must be the foundation of our <term>calculus</term> <note>Here meaning a method of calculation or a mathematical reasoning.</note>.

Therefore, at the time of the founding of *Stonehenge*, the variation was about the same quantity and place as about 1620 A.D. in our preceding table. Supposing with Dr. *Halley* that the revolution of this variation is about 700 years, three entire revolutions thereof bring us to about the year of the city <note>The author is likely referring to "Ab Urbe Condita," the years since the founding of Rome.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for William Stukeley's 1743 archaeological and theological study of Avebury, which he identifies as a Druidic temple. It introduces his theory that the British Isles were populated by practitioners of an ancient, "patriarchal" religion.</summary>
